(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{A})
measurable space and
f_{i}
measurable functions.

(D)

M. V. Tchakaloff 1957
7
7

7

Published: July-September, 1957

(
Tch 57
, Thm. II)
:
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathds{R}^{n}
compact and
f_{i}
monomials of degree at most
d
.

(E)

W. W. Rogosinski 1958
8
8

8

Received: August 22, 1957. Published: May 6, 1958.

(
Rog 58
, Thm. 1)
:
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{A})
measurable space and
f_{i}
measurable functions.

From this list we see that Tchakaloff’s result (
7
) from 1957 is a special case of Rosenbloom’s result (
8
) from 1952 and that the general case was proved by Richter and Rogosinski almost about at the same time, see the exact dates in the footnotes.
If one reads Richter’s paper, one might think at first glance that he treats only the one-dimensional case, but a closer look reveals that his Proposition (Satz) 4 covers actually the general case of measurable functions.
Rogosinski treats the one-dimensional case, but states at the end of the introduction of
Rog 58
:

Lastly, the restrictions in this paper to moment problems of dimension one is hardly essential.
Much of our geometrical arguments carries through, with obvious modifications, to any finite number of dimensions, and even to certain more general measure spaces.

The above proof of
3.10
, and likewise the one in
(
Sch 17
, Theorem 1.24)
, are nothing but modern formulations of the proofs of Richter and Rogosinski without additional arguments.
Note that Rogosinki’s paper
Rog 58
was submitted about a half year after the appearance of Richter’s
Ric 57
.

It might be of interest that the general results of Richter and Rogosinski from 1957/58 can be derived from Rosenbloom’s Theorem from 1952, see Problem
3
.
With that wider historical perspective in mind it might be justified to call
3.10
also the
Richter–Rogosinski–Rosenbloom Theorem
.

3.10
was overlooked in the modern literature on truncated polynomial moment problems.
The problem probably arose around 1997/98 when it was stated as an open problem in a published paper.
9
9

9

We do not give the references for this and subsequent papers who reproved
3.10
.

The paper
Ric 57
and numerous works of J. H. B. Kemperman were not included back then.
Especially
(
Kem 68
, Thm. 1)
where Kemperman fully states the general theorem (
3.10
) and attributed it therein to Richter and Rogosinski is missing.
Later on, this missing piece was not added in several other works.
The error continued in the literature for several years and
3.10
was reproved in several papers in weaker forms.
Even nowadays papers appear not aware of
3.10
or of the content of
Ric 57
.

20
Signed Representing Measures: Boas’ Theorem

In the theory of moments almost exclusively the representation by non-negative measures is treated.
The reason is the following result due to R. P. Boas from 1939.

Boas’ Theorem 3.11

(
Boa39b
or e.g.
(
ST 43
, p. 103, Thm. 3.11)
)

Let
s=(s_{i})_{i\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
be a real sequence. Then there exist infinitely many signed measures
\mu
on
\mathds{R}
and infinitely many signed measures
\nu
on
[0,\infty)
such that

s_{i}=\int_{\mathds{R}}x^{i}~\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{i}~\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

holds for all
i\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.

The proof follows the arguments in
(
ST 43
, pp. 103–104)
.

Proof.

We prove the case on
[0,\infty)
.
The case on
\mathds{R}
is then only a special case.

By induction we write
s=v-w
such that
v
and
w
are positive definite sequences where we can apply the
2.9
.

i=0
: We can chose
v_{0},w_{0}\gg 1
with
s_{0}=v_{0}-w_{0}
, i.e.,
L_{v}(p),L_{w}(p)\geq 0
for all
p\in\mathrm{Pos}([0,\infty))_{\leq 0}=[0,\infty)
.

i\to i+1
: Assume we found
(v_{j})_{j=0}^{i}
and
(w_{j})_{j=0}^{i}
such that
L_{v}(p),L_{w}(p)\geq 0
for all
p\in\mathrm{Pos}([0,\infty))_{\leq i}
.
Since for
i+1
the term
x^{i+1}
appears additionally to
1,x,x^{2},\dots,x^{i}
, the convex cone
\mathrm{Pos}([0,\infty))_{\leq i+1}
has compact base, and
L
is continuous on
\mathds{R}[x]_{\leq i+1}
we find
v_{i+1},w_{i+1}\gg 1
with
s_{i+1}=v_{i+1}-w_{i+1}
such that
L_{v}(p),L_{w}(p)\geq 0
for all
p\in\mathrm{Pos}([0,\infty))_{\leq i+1}
.

Hence, we found sequences
v,w
with
s=v-w
and
L_{v}(p),L_{w}(p)\geq 0
for all
p\in\mathrm{Pos}([0,\infty))
.
By the
2.9

L_{v}
is represented by some non-negative
\mu_{+}
and
L_{w}
is represented by some non-negative
\mu_{-}
both with support in
[0,\infty)
, i.e.,
L_{s}
is represented by
\mu=\mu_{+}-\mu_{-}
supported on
[0,\infty)
.
∎

T. Sherman showed that
3.11
(even when
L
is a complex linear functional) also holds in the
n
-dimensional case on
\mathds{R}^{n}
and
[0,\infty)^{n}
for any
n\in\mathds{N}

(
She 64
, Thm. 1)
.
Similar results are proved for linear functionals on the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{E}(G)
of a Lie group
G
by K. Schmüdgen
Sch 78
.
If the Lie group
G
is
\mathds{R}^{n}
then this again gives Sherman’s result.
G. Pólya
Pól 38
(see also
(
ST 43
, p. 104)
) showed an extension which kinds of measures can be chosen.
On
\mathds{R}^{n}
it is even possible to find a Schwartz function
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathds{R}^{n})
such that

s_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathds{R}^{n}}x^{\alpha}\cdot f(x)~\mathrm{d}x

for all
\alpha\in\mathds{N}_{0}^{n}
.
Use e.g.
CdD 22
.

3.11
also covers the case with gaps.
If any gaps in the real sequence
s
are present then fill them with any real number you like.

21
Solving all Truncated Moment Problems solves the Moment Problem

The following result was already indicated by Hamburger in
Ham 20
and formalized by J. Stochel in
Sto 01
.
We have the following.

Theorem 3.12

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
,
K\subseteq\mathds{R}^{n}
be closed,
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathds{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
be an adapted space on
K
, and let
L:\mathcal{V}\to\mathds{R}
be a linear functional on
\mathcal{V}
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

L:\mathcal{V}\to\mathds{R}
is a
K
-moment functional.

(ii)

L_{k}:=L|_{\mathcal{V}\cap\mathds{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]_{\leq k}}
are truncated
K
-moment functionals for all
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

While “(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)” is clear it is sufficient to prove the reverse direction.

Let
L_{k}
be a truncated
K
-moment functionals for all
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.
Since
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathds{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
for any
p\in\mathcal{V}
we have that
L:\mathcal{V}\to\mathds{R}
is well-defined by
L(p):=L_{\deg p}(p)
.
Let
p\in\mathcal{V}
with
p\geq 0
on
K
then
L(p)=L_{\deg p}(p)\geq 0
, i.e., by the
2.9
we have that
L
is a
K
-moment functional.
∎

Note,
\mathcal{V}
can also be finite dimensional when
K
is compact.
Then the result is trivial.
For unbounded
K
the adapted space
\mathcal{V}
is always infinite dimensional.

A more general version of
3.12
can e.g. be found in
(
Sch 17
, Thm. 1.20)
.

Problems

{prob}

Prove
3.1
with the
2.9
and the representation (
16
).

{prob}

Prove
3.2
with the
2.9
and the representation (
14
).

{prob}

Prove
3.3
with the
2.9
and the
3.7
, resp.
\mathrm{Pos}([a,b])
in (
21
).

{prob}

Prove
3.4
with the
2.9
.

{prob}

Use
3.5
(ii) to prove
3.6
.

{prob}

Prove
3.9
.

{prob}

Show that
3.10
follows from Rosenbloom’s Theorem, i.e., show that the additional assumption that all
f_{i}
are bounded on the measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{A})
can be removed.

Part II
Tchebycheff Systems

\motto

There is nothing more practical than a good theory.

Kurt Lewin
Lew 43

Chapter 4
T-Systems

In this chapter we introduce the Tchebycheff systems or short T-systems.
We give basic examples and properties.

22
The Early History of T-Systems

In our presentation we mostly limit ourselves to the works
Kre 51
;
Kar 63
;
KS 66
;
KN 77
.
However, the concept of T-system was introduces much earlier.
It goes back to its name giver: P. L. Tchebycheff
Tch 74
.
See especially
Kre 51
for a good overview of the history of the development of T-systems and also
Gon 00
.

In
Tch 74
Tchebycheff states the following open problem:

Let

a<\xi<\eta<b

be real numbers and let the numbers

s_{k}=\int_{a}^{b}x^{k}f(x)~\mathrm{d}x

(23)

for
k=0,1,\dots,n-1
for some
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
be given.
Find the bounds on the integral

\int_{\xi}^{\eta}f(x)~\mathrm{d}x

(24)

under the conditions that
f\geq 0
on
[a,b]
and (
23
) holds.

From this investigation Tchebycheff arrives at the method of continued fractions, which was used in the early results in the moment problems, see
Section

17
.
Tchebycheff gives without proof the inequalities (upper and lower bounds) of (
24
).
The proof was independently found by others, see
(
Kre 51
, pp. 3–4)
.
The key here is to work over a finitely dimensional space spanned by
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
.

A well-known and guiding example are the functions
1,x,\dots,x^{n}
.

Example 4.1
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
. Then the family
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system, see
4.2
below. This follows immediately from the Vandermonde determinant

\det\big(x_{i}^{j}\big)_{i,j=0}^{n}=\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{j}-x_{i})

for any
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
.
\circ

Krein states that he developed “the connection between ideas of Markov and functional-geometric ideas” which made it possible to remove the Wronskian approach (
5.6
) and replacing it with continuity and the condition

The curve
\Gamma
of the
(n+1)
-dimensional space
\mathds{R}^{n+1}
:

y_{0}=f_{0}(x),\quad y_{1}=f_{1}(x),\quad\dots,y_{n}=f_{n}(x)

does not intersect itself and no hyperplane through the origin intersects it in more than
n
points.

which is equivalent to

No linear combination

\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\quad\text{with}\quad\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}^{2}>0

vanishes more than
n
times in the closed interval
[a,b]
.

see
(
Kre 51
, pp. 19–20)
.
The later is then generalized to leave out continuity and replacing
[a,b]
with any set
\mathcal{X}
, see
4.2
.
For a family
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
with this property S. N. Bernstein
Ber 37
introduced the name
Tchebycheff system
and Krein
(
Kre 51
, p. 20)
and Archieser
(
Ach 56
, p. 73, §47)
continued using this terminology.

For more on the history see e.g.
Kre 51
.
We especially recommend the very nice survey article
Gon 00
with the references therein for more on the works, the contributions, and the impact of Tchebycheff’s work.

23
Definition and Basic Properties

Definition 4.2
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{X}
be a set with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
, and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a family of real functions
f_{i}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathds{R}
. We call a linear combination

f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f_{i}\quad\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}:=\{a_{0}f_{0}+\dots+a_{n}f_{n}\,|\,a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathds{R}\}

(25)

a
polynomial
.
The family
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathcal{X}
is called a
Tchebycheff system
(or short
T-system
)
of order
n
on
\mathcal{X}
if every polynomial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
with
\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}^{2}>0
has at most
n
zeros in
\mathcal{X}
.

If additionally
\mathcal{X}
is a topological space and
\mathcal{F}
is a family of continuous functions we call
\mathcal{F}
a
continuous T-system
.
If additionally
\mathcal{X}
is the unit circle
\mathds{T}
then we call
\mathcal{F}
a
periodic T-system
.

The following immediate consequence shows that we can restrict the domain
\mathcal{X}
of the T-system
\mathcal{F}
to some
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and as long as
|\mathcal{Y}|\geq n+1
the restricted T-system remains a T-system. In applications and examples we therefore only need to prove the T-system property on some larger set
\mathcal{X}
.

Corollary 4.3

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a T-system of order
n
on some set
\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
. Let
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{Y}|\geq n+1
. Then
\mathcal{G}:=\{f_{i}|_{\mathcal{Y}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system of order
n
on
\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof.

See Problem
4
.
∎

The set
\mathcal{X}
does not require any structure or property except
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
.

In the theory of T-systems we often deal with one special matrix.
We use the following abbreviation.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a family of real functions on a set
\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
. We define the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x_{0})&f_{1}(x_{0})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{0})\\
f_{0}(x_{1})&f_{1}(x_{1})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{1})\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{n})&f_{1}(x_{n})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{n})\end{pmatrix}=(f_{i}(x_{j}))_{i,j=0}^{n}

(26)

for any
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma 4.5

(see e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 31)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{X}
be a set with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
, and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a family of real functions
f_{i}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathds{R}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{F}
is a T-system of order
n
on
\mathcal{X}
.

(ii)

The determinant

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}

does not vanish for any pairwise distinct points
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii):
Let
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
be pairwise distinct.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system we have that any non-trivial polynomial
f
has at most
n
zeros, i.e., the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}

has trivial kernel and hence its determinant is non-zero. Since
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
are arbitrary pairwise distinct we have (ii).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Assume there is a polynomial
f
with
\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}^{2}>0
which has the
n+1
pairwise distinct zeros
z_{0},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
. Then the matrix

Z=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
z_{0}&z_{1}&\dots&z_{n}\end{pmatrix}

has non-trivial kernel since
0\neq(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})^{T}\in\ker Z
and hence
\det Z=0
in contradiction to (ii).
∎

4.5
is used in
(
KS 66
, p. 3, Dfn. 2.1)
as the definition of a continuous T-system where it is called a weak T-system.
In
(
KS 66
, p. 22, Thm. 4.1)
then the equivalence to
4.2
is shown.

Remark 4.6
.

4.5
implies that for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
there is a
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that
f(x)\neq 0
, i.e., the
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
do not have common zeros.
\circ

Remark 4.7
.

After adjusting the sign of
f_{n}
in a continuous T-system
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
on
[a,b]
we can assume that

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}>0

holds for all
a\leq x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{n}\leq b
.
\circ

The previous lemma implies the following.

Corollary 4.8

(see e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 33)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a T-system of order
n
on some set
\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
.
Let
\mathcal{W}
be a set with
n+1\leq|\mathcal{W}|\leq|\mathcal{X}|
and let
g:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{X}
be injective.
Then
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
with
g_{i}:=f_{i}\circ g
is a T-system of order
n
on
\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

See Problem
4
.
∎

Corollary 4.9

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 10)
or
(
KN 77
, p. 33)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a T-system of order
n
on some set
\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
.
Let
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathds{R}
be such that
g>0
on
\mathcal{X}
.
Then
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
with
g_{i}:=g\cdot f_{i}
is a T-system of order
n
on
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

See Problem
4
.
∎

Corollary 4.10

(see e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 33)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a T-system of order
n
on some set
\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
. The following hold:

(i)

The functions
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
are linearly independent over
\mathcal{X}
.

(ii)

For any
f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f_{i}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
the coefficients
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathds{R}
are unique.

Proof.

See Problem
4
.
∎

The previous corollary extends to the following result.

Theorem 4.11

(see e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 33)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{F}
be a T-system on some set
\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
, and let
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
be
n+1
pairwise different points.
The following hold:

(i)

Every
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
is uniquely determined by its values
f(x_{0}),\dots,f(x_{n})
.

(ii)

For any
y_{0},\dots,y_{n}\in\mathds{R}
there exists a unique
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that
f(x_{i})=y_{i}
holds for all
i=0,\dots,n
.

Proof.

(i):
Since
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
we have
f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f_{i}
.
Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
be pairwise distinct points. Then by
4.5
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) we have that

\begin{pmatrix}f(x_{0})\\
\vdots\\
f(x_{n})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{n}\end{pmatrix}

has the unique solution
\alpha_{0}=a_{0}
, …,
\alpha_{n}=a_{n}
.

(ii):
By the same argument as in (i) the system

\begin{pmatrix}y_{0}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{n}\end{pmatrix}

has the unique solution
\alpha_{0}=a_{0}
, …,
\alpha_{n}=a_{n}
.
∎

24
The Curtis–Mairhuber–Sieklucki Theorem

So far we imposed no structure on the set
\mathcal{X}
.
We now get a structure of
\mathcal{X}
.
The following structural result was proved in
(
Mai 56
, Thm. 2)
for compact subsets
\mathcal{X}
of
\mathds{R}^{n}
and for arbitrary compact sets
\mathcal{X}
in
Sie 58
and
(
Cur 59
, Thm. 8 and Cor.)
.

Curtis–Mairhuber–Sieklucki Theorem 4.12

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}
be a continuous T-system of order
n
on a topological space
\mathcal{X}
. If
\mathcal{X}
is a compact metrizable space then
\mathcal{X}
can be homeomorphically embedded in the unit circle
\{(x,y)\in\mathds{R}^{2}\,|\,x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}
.

The proof is not difficult but technical and too lengthy for our purposes.
We therefore refer the reader to
(
Cur 59
, Thm. 8)
.

An immediate consequence of the
4.12
is that every T-system is up to homomorphisms one-dimensional, i.e., in algebraic applications of the theory of T-systems we can only deal with the univariate case.
Additionally, we have the following result.

Corollary 4.13

(see e.g.
(
Cur 59
, Cor. after Thm. 8)
)

The order
n
of a periodic T-system is even.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:[0,2\pi]\to S=\{(x,y)\in\mathds{R}^{2}\,|\,x^{2}+y^{2}\}
with
\varphi(\alpha)=(\sin\alpha,\cos\alpha)
and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a periodic T-system. Then the
f_{i}
are continuous and hence also

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
t_{0}&t_{1}&\dots&t_{n}\end{pmatrix}

is continuous in
t_{0},\dots,t_{n}\in S
. If
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system we have that

d(\alpha):=\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
\varphi(\alpha)&\varphi(\alpha+2\pi/(n+1))&\dots&\varphi(\alpha+2n\pi/(n+1))\end{pmatrix}

in non-zero for all
\alpha\in[0,2\pi]
and never changes singes. If
n
is odd then
d(0)=-d(2\pi/(n+1))
which is a contradiction. Hence,
n
must be even.
∎

25
Examples of T-Systems

Example 4.14

(
4.1
continued)
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}=\mathds{R}
.
Then the family
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
of monomials is a T-system.
To see this let
x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}
be
n+1
points in
\mathds{R}
.
We then have by the Vandermonde determinant

\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x&\dots&x^{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{j}-x_{i})

(27)

which is always non-zero and hence
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system of order
n
on
\mathds{R}
by
4.5
. Additionally, by
4.3
we have that
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system of order
n
on any
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathds{R}
with
|\mathcal{Y}|\geq n+1
.
\circ

Note, that in (
27
) the functions
f_{i}
should be written more precisely as

f_{i}:\mathds{R}\to\mathds{R},\ x\mapsto x^{i}

and not just as
x^{i}
.
However, we then would have the notation

\begin{pmatrix}\cdot^{0}&\cdot^{1}&\dots&\cdot^{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{or more general}\quad\begin{pmatrix}\cdot^{\alpha_{0}}&\cdot^{\alpha_{1}}&\dots&\cdot^{\alpha_{n}}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}

for
\alpha_{i}
with
-\infty<\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}<\infty
which seems to be hard to read.
We will therefore abuse the notation and use
x^{i}
,
x^{\alpha_{i}}
, and (
27
).

4.14
can be generalized to non-negative real exponents.

Example 4.15

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 9, Exm. 1)
or
(
KN 77
, p. 38, §2(d))
)
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
0=\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
be non-negative reals.
Then

\mathcal{F}=\{x^{\alpha_{0}},x^{\alpha_{1}},\dots,x^{\alpha_{n}}\}

is a T-system of order
n
on any
\mathcal{X}\subseteq[0,\infty)
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
.
\circ

If we restrict
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{X}\subseteq(0,\infty)
then we can allow arbitrary real exponents
\alpha_{i}
.

Example 4.16
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
be reals. Then

\mathcal{F}=\left\{x^{\alpha_{0}},x^{\alpha_{1}},\dots,x^{\alpha_{n}}\right\}

is a T-system on any
\mathcal{X}\subseteq(0,\infty)
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
.
\circ

By using
\exp:\mathds{R}\to(0,\infty)
we find that the previous example is by
4.8
equivalent to the following.

Example 4.17

(see e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 38)
)
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
be reals. Then

\mathcal{G}=\left\{e^{\alpha_{0}x},e^{\alpha_{1}x},\dots,e^{\alpha_{n}x}\right\}

is a T-system on any
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathds{R}
with
|\mathcal{Y}|\geq n+1
.
\circ

That the equivalent Examples
4.16
and
4.17
are T-systems will be postponed to
5.18
.
The reason is that with the introduction of ET-systems in
Chapter

5
and especially
5.14
we generate plenty of examples of ET- and T-systems.

Example 4.18

(see e.g.
(
PS 64
, p. 41, no. 26)
or
(
KN 77
, p. 37-38)
)
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
be reals. Then

\mathcal{F}=\left\{\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{0}},\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{1}},\dots,\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{n}}\right\}

is a continuous T-system on any
[a,b]
or
[a,\infty)
with
-\alpha_{0}<a<b
.
\circ

Proof.

See Problem
4
.
∎

Example 4.19

(see e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 38)
)
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathds{R})
with
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
,
a<b
, and
f^{(n)}>0
on
\mathcal{X}
. Then

\mathcal{F}=\{1,x,x^{2},\dots,x^{n-1},f\}

is a continuous T-system of order
n
on
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
. We can also allow
\mathcal{X}=(a,b)
,
[a,\infty)
,
(-\infty,b)
, ….
\circ

With the techniques developed in
Chapter

5
it will be easy to show that
4.19
is not only a T-system but in fact also an ET- and ECT-system.
We will therefore postpone its proof to Problem
5
.

26
Representation as a Determinant, Zeros, and Non-Negativity

The following result shows that when enough zeros of a polynomial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
are known then
f
has the following representation as a determinant.

Theorem 4.20

(see e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 33)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
,
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a T-system on some set
\mathcal{X}
with
|\mathcal{X}|\geq n+1
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
be
n
pairwise distinct points, and let
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

f(x_{i})=0
holds for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
c\in\mathds{R}
such that

f(x)=c\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(28)

Proof.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Clear.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii):
If
f=0
then
c=0
so the assertion holds.
If
f\neq 0
then there exists a point
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
such that
f(x_{0})\neq 0
since
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system.
Then also the determinant in (ii) is non-zero and we can choose
c
such that both
f
and the scaled determinant coincide also in
x_{0}
.
By
4.10
a polynomial
f
is uniquely determined by its values
f(x_{i})
at
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}
.
This shows that (
28
) is the only polynomial which fulfills (i).
∎

So far we treated general T-systems.
For further properties we go to continuous T-systems.
By the
4.12
we can assume
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
.

Definition 4.21
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{F}
be a continuous T-system on
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
an interval,
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
, and let
x_{0}
be a zero of
f
. Then
x_{0}\in\mathrm{int}\,\mathcal{X}
is called a
non-nodal
zero if
f
does not change sign at
x_{0}
. Otherwise the zero
x_{0}
is called
nodal
, i.e., either
f
changes signs at
x_{0}
or
x_{0}
is a boundary point of
\mathcal{X}
.

The following result bounds the number of nodal and non-nodal zeros.

Theorem 4.22

(see
(
Kre 51
, Lem. 3.1)
or e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 34, Thm. 1.1)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{F}
be a continuous T-system of order
n
on
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
with
-\infty<a<b<\infty
.
If
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
has
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
non-nodal zeros and
l\in\mathds{N}_{0}
nodal zeros in
\mathcal{X}
then
2k+l\leq n
.

The proof is adapted from
(
KN 77
, pp. 34, Thm. 1.1)
.

Proof.

We make two case distinctions, one for
k=0
and one for
k\geq 1
.

k=0
: If
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
has
l
zeros then
l\leq n
by
4.2
.

k\geq 1
: Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}\in\mathrm{int}\,\mathcal{X}
with
p\leq k+l
be the zeros of
f
in
\mathrm{int}\,\mathcal{X}
. Set

M_{i}:=\max_{x_{i-1}\leq x\leq x_{i}}|f(x)|

for all
i=1,\dots,p+1
with
x_{0}=a
and
x_{p+1}=b
. Additionally, set

m:=\frac{1}{2}\min_{i=1,\dots,p+1}M_{i},

i.e.,
m>0
.

We construct a polynomial
g_{1}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that

(a)

g_{1}
has the value
g(x_{i})=m
at the non-nodal zeros
x_{i}
of
f
with
f\geq 0
in a neighborhood of
x_{i}
,

(b)

g_{1}
has the values
g(x_{i})=-m
at the non-nodal zeros
x_{i}
of
f
with
f\leq 0
in a neighborhood of
x_{i}
, and

(c)

g_{1}
vanishes at all nodal zeros
x_{i}
, i.e.,
g(x_{i})=0
.

After renumbering the zeros
x_{i}
we can assume
x_{1},\dots,x_{k_{1}}
fulfill (a),
x_{k_{1}+1},\dots,x_{k_{1}+k_{2}}
fulfill (b), and
x_{k_{1}+k_{2}+1},\dots,x_{k_{1}+k_{2}+l}
fulfill (c) with
k_{1}+k_{2}=k
. By
4.2
we have
k+l\leq n
and hence by
4.5
we have that

\begin{pmatrix}m\\
\vdots\\
m\\
-m\\
\vdots\\
-m\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x_{1})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{1})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{k_{1}})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{k_{1}})\\
f_{0}(x_{k_{1}+1})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{k_{1}+1})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{k})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{k})\\
f_{0}(x_{k+1})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{k+1})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{k+l})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{k+l})\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\beta_{n}\end{pmatrix}

(29)

has at least one solution, say
\beta_{0}=b_{0}
, …,
\beta_{n}=b_{n}
. Then
g_{1}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}\cdot f_{i}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
fulfills (a) to (c).

Set

\rho:=\frac{m}{2\cdot\|g_{1}\|_{\infty}}

and define
g_{2}:=f-g_{1}
.

We show that to each non-nodal zero
x_{i}
of
f
there correspond two zeros of
g_{2}
.
Let
x_{i}
be a non-nodal zero of
f
with
f\geq 0
in a neighborhood of
x_{i}
.
We can find a point
y_{i}\in(x_{i-1},x_{i})
and a point
y_{i+1}\in(x_{i},x_{i+1})
such that

f(y_{i})=M_{i}>m\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(y_{i+1})=M_{i+1}>m.

Hence,
g_{2}(y_{i})>0
and
g_{2}(y_{i+1})>0
. Since
g_{2}(x_{i})=-\rho\cdot m<0
it follows that
g_{2}
has a zero both in
(y_{i},x_{i})
and in
(x_{i},y_{i+1})
.

Additionally,
g_{2}
also vanishes at all nodal zeros of
f
and therefore has at least
2k+l
distinct zeros. By
4.2
we have
2k+l\leq n
.
∎

The previous result holds for more general sets
\mathcal{X}
.

Corollary 4.23

4.22
holds for sets
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
of the form

(i)

\mathcal{X}=(a,b)
,
[a,b)
,
(a,b]
with
-\infty<a<b<\infty
,

(ii)

\mathcal{X}=(a,\infty)
,
[a,\infty)
,
(-\infty,b)
,
(-\infty,b]
with
-\infty<a,b<\infty
,

(iii)

\mathcal{X}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}\subseteq\mathds{R}
with
k\geq n+1
and
x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
, and

(iv)

countable unions of (i) to (iii).

Proof.

\mathcal{X}=[0,\infty)
:
Let
0\leq x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
be the zeros of
f
in
[0,\infty)
.
Since every T-system on
[0,\infty)
is also a T-system on
[0,b]
for any
b>0
by
4.3
the assertion follows from
4.22
with
b=x_{k}+1
.

For the other assertions adapt (if necessary) the proof of
4.22
.
∎

That non-nodal points are always inner points and have a weight of (at least)
2
in counting with multiplicities as well as that boundary points are always non-nodal and are counted (at least) once in counting the multiplicities is generalized in the following.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
x\in[a,b]
with
a\leq b
. We define the
index

\varepsilon(x)
by

\varepsilon(x):=\begin{cases}2&\text{if}\ x\in(a,b),\\
1&\text{if}\ x=a\ \text{or}\ b.\end{cases}

(30)

The same definition holds for sets as in
4.23
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
be a set. We define the
index
\varepsilon(\mathcal{X})
of the set
\mathcal{X}
by

\varepsilon(\mathcal{X}):=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\varepsilon(x).

(31)

We now want to show that for each T-system
\mathcal{F}
not only non-negative polynomials
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
exists but we can even specify the zeros.
We need the following definition.

Definition 4.25
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}
be a T-system of order
n
on some set
\mathcal{X}
. We define

\displaystyle(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})^{e}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\;\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f_{i}\;\middle|\;\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}^{2}=1\;\right\},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}

\displaystyle:=\left\{f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\,\middle|\,f\geq 0\ \text{on}\ \mathcal{X}\right\},

and

\displaystyle(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}^{e}

\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})^{e}\cap(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}.

With these definitions we can prove the following existence criteria for non-negative polynomials in a T-systems on
[a,b]
.

Theorem 4.26

(see
(
Kre 51
, Lem. 3.2)
or e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 35, Thm. 1.2)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{F}
be a continuous T-system on
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
, and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in\mathcal{X}
be
m
distinct points for some
m\in\mathds{N}
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

The points
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
are zeros of a non-negative polynomial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
.

(ii)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon(x_{i})\leq n
.

The proof is adapted from
(
KN 77
, pp. 35, Thm. 1.2)
.

Proof.

“(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)” is
4.22
and we therefore only have to prove “(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)”.

Case I
: At first assume that
a<x_{1}<\dots<x_{m}<b
and
\sum_{i=0}^{m}\varepsilon(x_{i})=2m=n
.
If
2m<n
then add
k
additional points
x_{m+1},\dots,x_{m+k}
such that
2m+2k=n
and
x_{m}<x_{m+1}<\dots<x_{m+k}<b
.

Select a sequence of points
(x_{1}^{(j)},\dots,x_{m}^{(j)})\in\mathds{R}^{m}
,
j\in\mathds{N}
, such that

a<x_{1}<x_{1}^{(j)}<\dots<x_{m}<x_{m}^{(j)}<b

for all
j\in\mathds{N}
and
\lim_{j\to\infty}x_{i}^{(j)}=x_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,m
. Set

g_{j}(x):=c_{j}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}\\
x&x_{1}&x_{1}^{(j)}&\dots&x_{m}&x_{m}^{(j)}\end{pmatrix}\quad\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})^{e}

(32)

for some
c_{j}>0
.
Since
(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})^{e}
is compact we can assume that
g_{j}
converges to some
g_{0}\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})^{e}
.
Then
g_{0}
has
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
as zeros with
\varepsilon(x_{i})=2
and
g_{0}
is non-negative since
g_{j}>0
on
[a,x_{1})
,
(x_{1}^{(j)},x_{2})
, …,
(x_{m-1}^{(j)},x_{m})
, and
(x_{m}^{(j)},b]
as well as
g_{j}<0
on
(x_{1},x_{1}^{(j)})
,
(x_{2},x_{2}^{(j)})
, …,
(x_{m},x_{m}^{(j)})
.

Case II
:
If
a=x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{m}<b
with
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon(x_{i})=2m-1=n
the only modification required in case I is to replace (
32
) by

g_{j}(x):=-c_{j}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&\dots&f_{2m-2}&f_{2m-1}\\
x&a&x_{2}&x_{2}^{(j)}&\dots&x_{m}&x_{m}^{(j)}\end{pmatrix}\quad\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})^{e}

with some normalizing factor
c_{j}>0
.

Case III
:
The procedure is similar if
x_{m}=b
and
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon(x_{i})=n
.
∎

Remark 4.27
.

4.26
appears in
(
KN 77
, p. 35, Thm. 1.2)
in a stronger version, see also
(
Kre 51
, Lem. 3.4)
.

In
(
KN 77
, p. 35, Thm. 1.2)
and
(
Kre 51
, Lem. 3.4)
Krein claims that the
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
are the
only
zeros of some non-negative
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
.
This holds when
n=2m+2p
for some
p\geq 0
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in\mathrm{int}\,\mathcal{X}
.
To see this add to
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
in (
32
) points
x_{m+1},\dots,x_{m+p}\in\mathrm{int}\,\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
and get
g_{0}
.
Hence,
g_{0}\geq 0
has exactly the zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{m+p}
.
Then construct in a similar way
\tilde{g}_{0}
with the zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{m},\tilde{x}_{m+1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{m+p}
with
\tilde{x}_{m+1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{m+p}\in\mathrm{int}\,\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m+p}\}
.
Hence,
g_{0}+\tilde{g}_{0}\geq 0
has only the zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
.

A similar construction works for
n=2m+1
with or without end points
a
or
b
.
If
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
contains no end point, i.e., all
x_{i}\in\mathrm{int}\,\mathcal{X}
, then construct a
g_{0}
with an zero in
a
(and therefore
g_{0}(b)>0
since the index is odd) and a
\tilde{g}_{0}
with zero in
b
(and therefore
\tilde{g}_{0}(a)>0
).
Then
g_{0}+\tilde{g}_{0}
has no end point as a zero.

However, Krein misses that for
n=2m+2p
with
p\geq 0
and when one end point is contained in
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
then it might happen that also the other end point must appear.
In
(
KS 66
, p. 28, Thm. 5.1)
additional conditions are given which ensure that
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
are the only zeros of some
f\geq 0
.

For example if also
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n-1}
is a T-system then it can be ensured that
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
are the only zeros of some non-negative polynomial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
, see
(
KS 66
, p. 28, Thm. 5.1 (b-i))
, see Problem
4
.
For our main example(s), the algebraic polynomials with gaps, this holds.

The same problem appears in
(
KN 77
, p. 36, Thm. 1.3)
.
A weaker but correct version is given in
4.30
below.

4.22
with the condition that
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system
(
KS 66
, p. 28, Thm. 5.1)
is given below in
5.20
.

\circ

Remark 4.28
.

Assume that in
4.26
we have additionally that
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([a,b],\mathds{R})
. Then in (
32
) we can set
x_{i}^{(j)}=x_{i}+j^{-1}
for all
i=0,\dots,m
and
j\gg 1
. For
j\to\infty
with
c_{j}:=j^{m}
we then get

\displaystyle g_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}j^{m}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}\\
x&x_{1}&x_{1}+j^{-1}&\dots&x_{m}&x_{m}+j^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}j^{m}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x)&\dots&f_{2m}(x)\\
f_{0}(x_{1})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{1})\\
f_{0}(x_{1}+j^{-1})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{1}+j^{-1})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{m})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{m})\\
f_{0}(x_{m}+j^{-1})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{m}+j^{-1})\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x)&\dots&f_{2m}(x)\\
f_{0}(x_{1})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{1})\\
\frac{f_{0}(x_{1}+j^{-1})-f_{0}(x_{1})}{j^{-1}}&\dots&\frac{f_{2m}(x_{1}+j^{-1})-f_{2m}(x_{1})}{j^{-1}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{m})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{m})\\
\frac{f_{0}(x_{m}+j^{-1})-f_{0}(x_{m})}{j^{-1}}&\dots&\frac{f_{2m}(x_{m}+j^{-1})-f_{2m}(x_{m})}{j^{-1}}\end{pmatrix}

(33)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x)&\dots&f_{2m}(x)\\
f_{0}(x_{1})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{1})\\
f_{0}^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&f_{2m}^{\prime}(x_{1})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{m})&\dots&f_{2m}(x_{m})\\
f_{0}^{\prime}(x_{m})&\dots&f_{2m}^{\prime}(x_{m})\end{pmatrix},

i.e., a double zero at
x_{j}
is included by including the values
f_{i}^{\prime}(x_{j})
,
i=0,\dots,n
.
We will define that procedure and need these definitions for ET-systems in
Chapter

5
.

\circ

Corollary 4.29

4.26
also holds for intervals
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{X}=(a,b),\ (a,b],\ [a,b),\ [a,b],\ (a,\infty),\ [a,\infty),\ (-\infty,b),\ (-\infty,b],\ \text{and}\ \mathds{R}

(34)

with
a<b
.

Proof.

We have that “(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)” follows from
4.23
.
For “(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)” we apply
4.26
on
[\min_{i}x_{i},\max_{i}x_{i}]
.
∎

We will now give a sharper version of
4.22
, see also
4.27
.

Theorem 4.30

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 30, Thm. 5.2)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\mathcal{F}
be a continuous T-system on
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
.
Additionally, let
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathcal{X}
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{l}\in\mathcal{X}
be pairwise distinct points.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

There exists a polynomial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that

(a)

x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
are the non-nodal zeros of
f
and

(b)

y_{1},\dots,y_{l}
are the nodal zeros of
f
.

(ii)

2k+l\leq n
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): That is
4.22
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Adapt the proof and especially the
g_{j}
’s in (
32
) of
4.26
accordingly.
Let
z_{1}<\dots<z_{k+l}
be the
x_{i}
’s and
y_{i}
’s together ordered by size.
Then in
g_{j}
treat every nodal
z_{i}
like the endpoint
a
or
b
, i.e., include it only once in the determinant, and insert for every non-nodal point
z_{i}
the point
z_{i}
and the sequence
z_{i}^{(j)}\in(z_{i},z_{i+1})
with
\lim_{j\to\infty}z_{i}^{(j)}=z_{i}
.
∎

Corollary 4.31

4.30
also holds for sets
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
of the form

(i)

\mathcal{X}=(a,b)
,
[a,b)
,
(a,b]
with
a<b
,

(ii)

\mathcal{X}=(a,\infty)
,
[a,\infty)
,
(-\infty,b)
,
(-\infty,b]
,

(iii)

\mathcal{X}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}\subseteq\mathds{R}
with
k\geq n+1
and
x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
, and

(iv)

finitely many unions of (i) to (iii).

Proof.

In the adapted proof and the
g_{j}
’s in (
32
) of
4.26
we do not need to have non-negativity, i.e., in the
g_{j}
’s sign changes at the
y_{i}
’s are allowed (and even required).
∎

) have at least one solution?

{prob}

Assume in
4.26
we not only have that
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system of order
n
, but additionally that
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n-1}
is T-systems of order
n-1
.
Then show that the following are equivalent:

(i)

The distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}\in[a,b]
are the
only
zeros of some non-negative polynomial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
.

(ii)

\sum_{i=1}^{p}\varepsilon(x_{i})\leq n
.

Curiouser and curiouser!

Lewis Carroll: Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland

Chapter 5
ET- and ECT-Systems

In this chapter we introduce the concept of ET- and ECT-systems, i.e.,
extended
and
extended complete
Tchebycheff systems.
The sparse algebraic polynomial systems on
(0,\infty)
are the main examples.
Being an ET-system is required for certain Positiv- and Nichtnegativstellensätze in later chapters.

27
Definitions and Basic Properties

We remind the reader that a function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathds{R},\mathds{R})
has a
zero
at
x_{0}\in\mathds{R}

of multiplicity
(at least)
m
if

f^{(k)}(x_{0})=0\qquad\text{for all}\ k=0,1,\dots,m-1.

(35)

For univariate polynomials
f\in\mathds{R}[x]
this translates into a factorization

f(x)=(x-x_{0})^{m}\cdot g(x)\qquad\text{for some}\ g\in\mathds{R}[x].

(36)

While the concept of T-systems comes from the univariate polynomials, a relation like (
36
) is in general not accessible for T-systems.
Hence, we rely on the more general (analytic) notion (
35
) of multiplicity but still call it
algebraic multiplicity
.
At endpoints of intervals
[a,b]
we use of course the one-sided derivatives.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be a T-system of order
n
on
[a,b]
with
a<b
.
\mathcal{F}
is called an
extended Tchebycheff system
(short
ET-system
)
on
[a,b]
if any polynomial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\setminus\{0\}
has at most
n
zeros in
[a,b]
counting algebraic multiplicities.

Remark 5.2
.

It is clear that every ET-system is also a T-system by only allowing multiplicity one for each zero.
\circ

In
4.28
eq. (
33
) we showed how double zeros can be included in the determinantal representation.
Whenever we have
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-functions in
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
and

x_{0}<\dots<x_{i}=x_{i+1}<\dots<x_{n}

we define

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&\dots&f_{i-1}&f_{i}&f_{i+1}&f_{i+2}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&\dots&x_{i-1}&(x_{i}&x_{i})&x_{i+2}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x_{0})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{0})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{i-1})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{i-1})\\
f_{0}(x_{i})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{i})\\
f_{0}^{\prime}(x_{i})&\dots&f_{n}^{\prime}(x_{i})\\
f_{0}(x_{i+2})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{i+2})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{n})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{n})\end{pmatrix}

(37)

and equivalently when
x_{j}=x_{j+1}
,
x_{k}=x_{k+1}
, … for additional entries.

We use the additional brackets “
(
” and “
)
” to indicate that
x_{i}
is inserted in the
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
and then also into
f_{0}^{\prime},\dots,f_{n}^{\prime}
to distinguish (
37
) from
4.4
to avoid confusion. Hence, in
4.4
we have

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&\dots&f_{i-1}&f_{i}&f_{i+1}&f_{i+2}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&\dots&x_{i-1}&x_{i}&x_{i}&x_{i+2}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}=0

since in two rows
x_{i}
is inserted into
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
, while in (
37
) we have that

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&\dots&f_{i-1}&f_{i}&f_{i+1}&f_{i+2}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&\dots&x_{i-1}&(x_{i}&x_{i})&x_{i+2}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}

indicates that
x_{i}
is inserted in
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
and then also into
f_{0}^{\prime},\dots,f_{n}^{\prime}
.

Extending this to zeros of multiplicity
m
for
\mathcal{C}^{m-1}
-functions is straight forward and we leave it to the reader to write down the formulas.
Similar to (
37
) we write for any
a\leq x_{0}\leq x_{1}\leq\dots\leq x_{n}\leq b
the matrix as

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}^{*}

(38)

when
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
are sufficiently differentiable.

We often want to express polynomials
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
as determinants (
32
) only by knowing their zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
.
If arbitrary multiplicities appear we only have
x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\dots\leq x_{n}
where we include zeros multiple times according to their algebraic multiplicities.
Hence, for

x_{0}=\dots=x_{i_{1}}\;<\;x_{i_{1}+1}=\dots=x_{i_{2}}\;<\;\dots\;<\;x_{i_{k}+1}=\dots=x_{n}

we introduce a simpler notation to write down (
37
):

\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}f_{0}&\,f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x&\,x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{n}\end{array}\right):=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}\;\dots\;f_{i_{1}}&f_{i_{1}+1}\;\dots\;f_{i_{2}}&\dots&f_{i_{k}+1}\;\dots\;f_{i_{k}+1}\\
x&(x_{1}\;\dots\;x_{i_{1}})&(x_{i_{1}+1}\;\dots\;x_{i_{2}})&\dots&(x_{i_{k}+1}\;\dots\;\;\;x_{n})\;\,\end{pmatrix}.

(39)

Clearly
(\ref{eq:doublezeroDfn2})\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
. For (
39
) to be well-defined we need
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{m-1}
where
m
is the largest multiplicity of any zero.

We see here why we require in
5.1

\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
.
In the case of
x_{0}=x_{1}=\dots=x_{n}
the functions
f_{i}
need to be
\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
, not just
\mathcal{C}^{n-1}([a,b],\mathds{R})
.

Similar to
4.5
we have the following.

Theorem 5.3

(
Kre 51
or e.g.
(
KN 77
, p. 37, P.1.1)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
with
a<b
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system.

(ii)

We have

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}^{*}\neq 0

for every
a\leq x_{0}\leq x_{1}\leq\dots\leq x_{n}\leq b
.

Proof.

Let
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in[a,b]
with

a\;\leq\;x_{0}=\dots=x_{i_{1}}\;<\;x_{i_{1}+1}=\dots=x_{i_{2}}\;<\;\dots\;<\;x_{i_{k}}=\dots=x_{n}\;\leq\;b

be the zeros of some
f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{n}f_{i}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
.
We get the coefficients
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}
from the system

0=\begin{pmatrix}f(x_{0})\\
f^{\prime}(x_{0})\\
\vdots\\
f^{(i_{1})}(x_{0})\\
f(x_{i_{1}+1})\\
\vdots\\
f^{(n-i_{k})}(x_{i_{k}})\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}^{*}}_{=:M}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}\\
a_{1}\\
\vdots\\
a_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(40)

Hence, since
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}
are arbitrary we have (i)
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-systems
\Leftrightarrow a_{0}=\dots=a_{n}=0\Leftrightarrow
(
40
) has only the trivial solution
\Leftrightarrow

M
has full rank
\Leftrightarrow
(ii).
∎

Remark 5.4
.

Similar to
4.7
for T-systems we can assume after a sign change in
f_{n}
that for every ET-system
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
on
[a,b]
we have that

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}^{*}>0

holds for all
a\leq x_{0}\leq x_{1}\leq\dots\leq x_{n}\leq b
since
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
.
\circ

An even more special case of ET-systems and therefore T-systems are the ECT-systems which we define now.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
with
a<b
. The family
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{0}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is called an
extended complete Tchebycheff system
(short
ECT-system
)
on
[a,b]
if
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
is an ET-system on
[a,b]
for all
k=0,\dots,n
.

28
Wronskian Determinant

To handle and work with ECT-systems it is useful to introduce the following determinant.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be with
a<b
.
For each
k=0,\dots,n
we define the
Wronskian determinant
(short
Wronskian
)
\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})
of
f_{0},\dots,f_{k}
to be

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},f_{1},\dots,f_{k}):=\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{0}^{\prime}&\dots&f_{0}^{(k)}\\
f_{1}&f_{1}^{\prime}&\dots&f_{1}^{(k)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{k}&f_{k}^{\prime}&\dots&f_{k}^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}.

(41)

The Wronskian is a common tool in the theory of ordinary differential equations.

In the previous definition (
41
) we could also shortly write

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})(x):=\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{k}\\
x&x&\dots&x\end{pmatrix}^{*}

for all
x\in[a,b]
.

Let
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\in\mathds{N}
with
m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}=n+1
and
x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
. Then the first
m_{j}
columns of
\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})
are the
m_{j}
columns in

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&\dots&f_{m_{1}-1}&f_{m_{1}}&\dots&f_{m_{1}+m_{2}-1}&f_{m_{1}+m_{2}}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{1}&\dots&x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{2}&x_{3}&\dots&x_{k}\end{pmatrix}^{*}

involving
x_{j}
.

Lemma 5.7

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be an ET-system on
[a,b]
with
a<b
, and let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
with
g>0
.
Then

\mathcal{G}:=\{g_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}\quad\text{with}\quad g_{i}:=g\cdot f_{i}

is an ET-system and we have

\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{n})=g^{n+1}\cdot\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n}).

Proof.

See Problem
5
.
∎

Lemma 5.8

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be an ET-system on
[c,d]
, and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],[c,d])
with
g^{\prime}>0
on
[a,b]
.
Then

\mathcal{G}:=\{f_{i}\circ g\}_{i=0}^{n}\quad\text{with}\quad g_{i}:=f_{i}\circ g

is an ET-system on
[a,b]
with

\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{n})=(g^{\prime})^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}\cdot\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})\circ g.

Proof.

See Problem
5
.
∎

For the Wronskian the following reduction property holds.

Lemma 5.9

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 377)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be with
a<b
and
f_{0}>0
.
Then for the
reduced system

g_{0},\dots,g_{n-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{n-1}([a,b],\mathds{R})
defined by

g_{i}:=\left(\frac{f_{i+1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}

(42)

for all
i=0,\dots,n-1
we have

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})=f_{0}^{n+1}\cdot\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{n-1}).

(43)

Proof.

See Problem
5
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Since
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
we have that
\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})(x)
is continuous in
x\in[a,b]
and hence after adjusting the signs of
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
we have that (
41
) being non-zero on
[a,b]
is equivalent to
\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})>0
on
[a,b]
for all
k=0,\dots,n
, see also
4.7
and
5.4
.
\circ

Lemma 5.11

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, pp. 242–245, Lem. 5.1 - 5.3)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be such that

\mathcal{W}(f_{0})>0,\quad\dots,\quad\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})>0

on
[a,b]
.
Define functions
g_{0},\dots,g_{n}:[a,b]\to\mathds{R}
by

\displaystyle g_{0}

\displaystyle:=f_{0}

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle:=D_{0}f_{1}

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle:=D_{1}D_{0}f_{2}

\displaystyle\ \;\;\vdots

\displaystyle g_{n}

\displaystyle:=D_{n-1}\dots D_{1}D_{0}f_{n}

with

D_{j}f:=\left(\frac{f}{g_{j}}\right)^{\prime},\quad i.e.,\quad D_{j}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{1}{g_{j}}.

(44)

Then

(i)

g_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{n-i}([a,b],\mathds{R})
are well defined with

g_{1}=\frac{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},f_{1})}{f_{0}^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{i}=\frac{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{i})\cdot\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{i-2})}{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{i-1})^{2}}

for all
i=2,\dots,n
,

(ii)

g_{i}>0
on
[a,b]
for all
i=0,\dots,n
,

(iii)

for any
g_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
with
g_{n+1}>0
on
[a,b]
we define

f_{n+1}(x):=g_{0}(x)\int_{a}^{x}g_{1}(y_{1})\int_{a}^{y_{1}}g_{2}(y_{2})\dots\int_{a}^{y_{n}}g_{n+1}(y_{n+1})~\mathrm{d}y_{n+1}\dots\mathrm{d}y_{1}

and we get

g_{n+1}=D_{n}\dots D_{1}D_{0}f_{n+1},

(iv)

for all
k=0,\dots,n+1
we have

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})=g_{0}^{k+1}g_{1}^{k}\cdots g_{k}

with
g_{n+1}
and
f_{n+1}
from (
iii
),

(v)

there exists a
f_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}^{n+1}([a,b],\mathds{R})
such that

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1})>0

on
[a,b]
, and

(vi)

for all
k=0,\dots,n+1
the families
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
are T-systems on
[a,b]
.

Proof.

(i) and (ii): Since
\mathcal{W}(f_{0})>0
we have
f_{0}>0
and hence
g_{1}=(f_{1}/f_{0})^{\prime}
is well-defined and we have

\frac{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},f_{1})}{f_{0}^{2}}=f_{0}^{-2}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{0}^{\prime}\\
f_{1}&f_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{f_{0}f_{1}^{\prime}-f_{1}f_{0}^{\prime}}{f_{0}^{2}}=\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}=g_{1},

i.e.,
g_{1}>0
on
[a,b]
.
The relations for
g_{i}
for all
i=2,\dots,n
follow by induction from Sylvester’s identity
Syl 51
;
AAM 96
.

(iii): From the definition of
f_{n+1}
we get immediately
g_{n+1}=D_{n}\dots D_{1}D_{0}f_{n+1}
.

(iv): Follows immediately from (i).

(v): Take the
f_{n+1}
from (iii).

(vi): For
k=0
it is clear that
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{0}
is a T-system since
f_{0}>0
on
[a,b]
.
So assume that for any
f_{0},\dots,f_{n-1}
with
\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})>0
on
[a,b]
for all
k=0,\dots,n-1
we have that all
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
with
k=0,\dots,n-1
are T-systems.
We show that
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is also a T-system.
So let
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in[a,b]
with
x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}
.
We then have

\displaystyle\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\det(f_{i}(x_{j}))_{i,j=0}^{n}

and factoring out
f_{0}(x_{j})>0
in each column gives

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{n}f_{0}(x_{j})\cdot\det\left(\tilde{f}_{i}(x_{j})\right)_{i,j=0}^{n}

with
\tilde{f}_{i}:=f_{i}/f_{0}
for all
i=0,\dots,n
and substracting from each row its predecessor (the row above) gives

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{n}f_{0}(x_{j})\cdot\det\left(\delta_{0,j},\tilde{f}_{1}(x_{j})-\tilde{f}_{1}(x_{j-1}),\dots,\tilde{f}_{n}(x_{j})-\tilde{f}_{n}(x_{j-1})\right)_{j=0}^{n}.

Expanding along the first column and applying the theorem of the mean gives

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{n}f_{0}(x_{j})\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(x_{i+1}-x_{i})\cdot\det\left(\hat{f}_{i}(y_{j})\right)_{i,j=0}^{n-1}

for some
y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1}
with
x_{0}<y_{0}<x_{1}<y_{1}<\dots<y_{n-1}<x_{n}
and
\hat{f}_{i}:=(f_{i+1}/f_{0})^{\prime}
for all
i=0,\dots,n-1
.
The family
\{\hat{f}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n-1}
is the reduced system from
5.9
and hence by (
43
) we have

\mathcal{W}(\hat{f}_{0},\dots,\hat{f}_{k-1})=\frac{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})}{f_{0}^{k+1}}>0

on
[a,b]
for all
k=1,\dots,n
.
By the induction hypothesis we have that
\{\hat{f}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n-1}
is a T-system, i.e.,

\det\left(\hat{f}_{i}(y_{j})\right)_{i,j=0}^{n-1}\neq 0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}\neq 0

and
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system which ends the proof.
∎

The previous lemma is used to characterize all ECT-systems.

29
Characterizations of ECT-Systems

We have the following characterization of ECT-systems.

Theorem 5.12

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 376, Thm. 1.1)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be with
a<b
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is an ECT-system.

(ii)

For all
k=0,\dots,n
we have that
\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})\neq 0
on
[a,b]
.

After adjusting the signs of
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
by
5.10
we can in
5.12
(ii) also assume that
\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})>0
on
[a,b]
for all
k=0,\dots,n
.

The following proof is adapted from
(
KS 66
, pp. 376–379)
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): Since every ECT-system is also an ET-system the statement is
5.3
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) because

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})(x)=\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{k}\\
x&x&\dots&x\end{pmatrix}^{*}

for all
x\in[a,b]
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): To show that
\mathcal{F}
is an ECT-system we have to show that
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
is an ET-system for all
k=0,\dots,n
.
And to show that
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
is an ET-system it is by
5.3
sufficient to show

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{k}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{k}\end{pmatrix}^{*}\neq 0

for every
a\leq x_{0}\leq x_{1}\leq\dots\leq x_{k}\leq b
.
We make two case distinctions:

Case I:

All
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
are pairwise distinct:
x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}
.

Case II:

At least once we have
x_{j}=x_{j+1}
for some
j=0,\dots,n-1
.

After renaming
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
we can assume
a\leq x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{l}\leq b
and
m_{1},\dots,m_{l}\in\mathds{N}
are the algebraic multiplicities with
m_{1}+\dots+m_{l}=n+1
for some
l\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.

Case I:
We have
m_{0}=\dots=m_{k}=1
and that is
5.11
(
vi
).

Case II:
We assume
m_{j}\geq 2
for some
j
. We show that we can reduce the system.

We show this reduction by induction over
n
.

Induction beginning
(
n=0
): Since
\mathcal{W}(f_{0})(x)\neq 0
it is an ET- and an ECT-system.
We can assume by changing the sign of
f_{0}
that
f_{0}>0
on
[a,b]
.

Induction step
(
n-1\to n
): By the induction beginning (
n=0
) we can assume
f_{0}>0
on
[a,b]
.
Then we have to show that

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{m_{1}-1}&f_{m_{1}}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{1}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{l}\end{pmatrix}^{*}

(45)

is non-zero.
To show this we factor
f_{0}(x_{j})>0
out of the
m_{j}
rows containing
x_{j}
in (
45
) for each
j=0,\dots,l
to get

\det\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{f_{0}^{\prime}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\dots&\frac{f_{0}^{(m_{1}-1)}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&1&\dots&\frac{f_{0}^{(m_{l}-1)}}{f_{0}}(x_{l})\\
\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{1}^{\prime}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\dots&\frac{f_{1}^{(m_{1}-1)}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}(x_{2})&\dots&\frac{f_{1}^{(m_{l}-1)}}{f_{0}}(x_{l})\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\dots&\frac{f_{n}^{(m_{1}-1)}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}(x_{2})&\dots&\frac{f_{n}^{(m_{l}-1)}}{f_{0}}(x_{l})\end{pmatrix}.

Then subtract from each of the columns containing
x_{j}
a linear combination of its predecessors to obtain for these
m_{j}
columns the first
m_{j}
columns of
\mathcal{W}(1,f_{1}/f_{0},\dots,f_{n}/f_{0})
evaluated at
x_{j}
:

\det\begin{pmatrix}1&\left(\frac{f_{0}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{0}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&1&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{0}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\\
\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\end{pmatrix}\\
=\det\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\dots&0&1&\dots&0\\
\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\end{pmatrix}.

The Leibniz rule on differentiation, here for us explicitly

\left(\frac{f_{i}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(k)}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\cdot f_{i}^{(k-j)}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{f_{0}}\right)^{(j)},

ensures that this is always possible.

We then subtract from each column which starts with a
1
its predecessor which also starts with a
1
and apply the mean value theorem to get apart from the positive factor
(x_{j+1}-x_{j})

\det\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\dots&0&0&0&\dots&0\\
\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(y_{2})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(y_{2})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\end{pmatrix}

with
x_{1}<y_{2}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{l}
and expanding by the first row gives

\det\begin{pmatrix}\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(y_{2})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{1})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{1}-1)}(x_{1})&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(y_{2})&\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x_{2})&\dots&\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right)^{(m_{l}-1)}(x_{l})\end{pmatrix}.

(46)

In (
46
) we now have the reduced system
g_{i}:=(f_{i+1}/f_{0})^{\prime}
with
i=0,\dots,n-1
from (
42
) in
5.9
.
By (
43
) in
5.9
and since the reduced systems is of dimension
n-1
where the inductions hypotheses holds we have that (
46
) is non-zero and hence also (
45
) is non-zero which we wanted to prove.
∎

Remark 5.13

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 379, Rem. 1.2)
)
.

We find the following complete characterization of ECT-systems which requires the additional property (
47
).
Fortunately, this seemingly additional property can always be generated by a change of basis vectors, i.e., for any vector space spanned by an ECT-system a suitable basis with (
47
) can be found.
\circ

Theorem 5.14

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 379, Thm. 1.2)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be such that

f_{j}^{(k)}(a)=0

(47)

holds for all
k=0,\dots,j-1
and
j=1,\dots,n
.
After suitable sign changes in
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
the following are equivalent:

(i)

There exist
g_{0},\dots,g_{n}
with
g_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{n-i}([a,b],\mathds{R})
and
g_{i}>0
on
[a,b]
for all
i=0,\dots,n
such that

\displaystyle f_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=g_{0}(x)

\displaystyle f_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=g_{0}(x)\cdot\int_{a}^{x}g_{1}(y_{1})~\mathrm{d}y_{1}

\displaystyle f_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=g_{0}(x)\cdot\int_{a}^{x}g_{1}(y_{1})\cdot\int_{a}^{y_{1}}g_{2}(y_{2})~\mathrm{d}y_{2}~\mathrm{d}y_{1}

\displaystyle\;\,\vdots

\displaystyle f_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=g_{0}(x)\cdot\int_{a}^{x}g_{1}(y_{1})\cdot\int_{a}^{y_{1}}g_{2}(y_{2})~{\dots}\int_{a}^{y_{n-1}}g_{n}(y_{n})~\mathrm{d}y_{n}~\dots~\mathrm{d}y_{2}~\mathrm{d}y_{1}.

(ii)

\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is an ECT-system on
[a,b]
.

(iii)

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})>0
on
[a,b]
for all
k=0,\dots,n
.

If one and therefore all of the equivalent conditions (i) – (iii) hold then the
g_{i}
in (i) are given by

g_{0}:=f_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{i}:=D_{i-1}\dots D_{1}D_{0}f_{i}\quad\text{with}\quad D_{i}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{1}{f_{0}}

for all
i=1,\dots,n
or equivalently by

g_{0}:=f_{0},\quad g_{1}:=\frac{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},f_{1})}{f_{0}^{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad g_{i}:=\frac{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{i})\cdot\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{i-2})}{\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{i-1})^{2}}

for all
i=2,\dots,n
.

Proof.

“(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii)” is
5.12
, “(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)” is
5.11
(i) – (iii), and
“(i)
\Rightarrow
(iii)”
is
5.11
(iv).
∎

Condition (ii) in
5.14
is of course to be understood after suitable sign changes in
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
.

The partial statement
5.14
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) can be found e.g. in
(
KS 66
, p. 19, Exm. 12)
and
(
KN 77
, pp. 39–40, P.2.4)
.

30
Examples of ET- and ECT-Systems

An equivalent result as
4.3
for T-systems, i.e., restricting the domain
\mathcal{X}
of a T-system leads again to a T-system, also holds for ET- and ECT-systems.
We leave that to the reader, see Problem
5
.
Hence, it is sufficient to give (examples of) ET- and ECT-systems with the largest possible domain
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathds{R}
.

While the condition of being an ET-system or being even an ECT-system seems very restrictive, several examples are known.

Example 5.15
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathds{R}
is an ECT-system.
\circ

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathds{R},\mathds{R})
and every non-trivial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}=\mathds{R}[x]_{\leq n}
has at most
n
real zeros counting multiplicities by the fundamental theorem of algebra, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-systems. Besides that we have that

\mathcal{W}(1,x,x^{2},\dots,x^{k})(x)=\det\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&\dots&0\\
x&1&0&\dots&0\\
x^{2}&2x&2&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
x^{k}&kx^{k}&k(k-1)x^{k-1}&\dots&k!\end{pmatrix}\geq 1

holds for all
x\in\mathds{R}
and
k=0,\dots,n
which shows that
\mathcal{F}
is also an ECT-system.
∎

Example 5.16
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\{1,x,x^{3}\}
on
[0,b]
with
b>0
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system (see
4.15
) but not an ET-system.
To see this let
x_{0}=x_{1}=x_{2}=0
, then

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

This shows that
\mathcal{F}
is not an ET-system.
\circ

In the previous example the position
x=0
prevents the T-system to be an ET-system.
If
x=0
is removed then it is even an ECT-system.

Example 5.17
.

Let
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathds{N}_{0}
with
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
.
Then
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{\alpha_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
on
(0,\infty)
is an ECT-system.
For
n=2m
and
0<x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{m}
we often encounter a specific polynomial structure and hence we write it down explicitly once:

\displaystyle\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{0}}&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}&x^{\alpha_{2m}}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-m}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{0}}&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}&x^{\alpha_{2m}}\\
x&x_{1}&x_{1}+\varepsilon&\dots&x_{m}&x_{m}+\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x_{i}-x)(x_{i}+\varepsilon-x)\right]\cdot\left[\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}(x_{j}-x_{i})^{2}(x_{j}-x_{i}-\varepsilon)(x_{j}+\varepsilon-x_{i})\right]

(48)

\displaystyle\qquad\times s_{\alpha}(x,x_{1},x_{1}+\varepsilon,\dots,x_{m},x_{m}+\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x_{i}-x)^{2}\cdot\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}(x_{j}-x_{i})^{4}\cdot s_{\alpha}(x,x_{1},x_{1},\dots,x_{m},x_{m})

where
s_{\alpha}
is the Schur polynomial of
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n})

Mac 95
.
Hence,

s_{\alpha}(x,x_{1},x_{1},\dots,x_{m},x_{m})

is not divisible by any
(x_{i}-x)
.

\circ

Proof.

Combine the induction

f^{(m+1)}(x)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{f^{(m)}(x+h)-f^{(m)}(x)}{h}

and

\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{0}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{n}}\\
x_{0}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{j}-x_{i})\cdot s_{\alpha}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})

where
s_{\alpha}
is the Schur polynomial of
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n})
.
∎

With
5.14
the previous example can be generalized.

Examples 5.18

(Examples
4.16
and
4.17
continued)
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let

-\infty<\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}<\infty

be reals.
Then

(a)

\mathcal{F}=\{x^{\alpha_{0}},\dots,x^{\alpha_{n}}\}

on
\mathcal{X}=(0,\infty)
(
4.16
) and

(b)

\mathcal{G}=\{e^{\alpha_{0}x},\dots,e^{\alpha_{n}x}\}

on
\mathcal{Y}=\mathds{R}
(
4.17
)

are ECT-systems.
\circ

Proof.

See Problem
5
.
∎

In Problem
5
we will see that also
4.19
are ET- and ECT-systems.

31
Representation as a Determinant, Zeros, and Non-Negativity

Similar to
4.20
we have the following for ET-systems, i.e., knowing
n
zeros of a polynomial
f
counting multiplicities determines
f
uniquely up to a scalar.

Theorem 5.19

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{n}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be an ET-system.
Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in[a,b]
with

x_{1}=\dots=x_{i_{1}}\;<\;x_{i_{1}+1}=\dots=x_{i_{1}+i_{2}}\;<\;\dots\;<\;x_{i_{1}+\dots+i_{k-1}+1}=\dots=x_{i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}=n}

for some
k,i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in\mathds{N}
and let
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

f^{(l)}(x_{j})=0
for all
j=1,\dots,k
and
l=0,\dots,i_{j}-1
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
c\in\mathds{R}
such that

f(x)=c\cdot\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}f_{0}&\,f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x&\,x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{n}\end{array}\right).

Proof.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Clear.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii):
If
f=0
then
c=0
so the assertion holds.
If
f\neq 0
then there exists a point
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
such that
f(x_{0})\neq 0
since
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system.
Then also the determinant in (ii) is non-zero and we can choose
c
such that both
f
and the scaled determinant coincide also in
x_{0}
.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system we have by
5.3
that

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}^{*}

has full rank, i.e., the coefficients of
f
and

c\cdot\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}f_{0}&\,f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x&\,x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{n}\end{array}\right)

coincide.
∎

The following result is a strengthened version of
4.26
.
It is a small extension of e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 28, Thm. 5.1)
with explicit multiplicities of the zeros of a non-negative polynomial.

Theorem 5.20

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be an ET-system on
[a,b]
with
a<b
.
Let
x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
in
[a,b]
and let
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\in\mathds{N}
for some
k\in\mathds{N}
.
The following hold:

(a)

If
m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}\leq n
and
m_{i}\in 2\mathds{N}
for all
x_{i}\in(a,b)
then there exists a
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that

(i)

f\geq 0
on
[a,b]
,

(ii)

f
has precisely the zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
,

(iii)

the zeros
x_{i}\in(a,b)
of
f
have multiplicity
m_{i}
,

(iv)

if
x_{1}=a
then
x_{1}=a
has multiplicity
m_{1}
or
m_{1}+1
, and

(v)

if
x_{k}=b
then
x_{k}=b
has multiplicity
m_{k}
or
m_{k}+1
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{F}
is an ECT-system or
m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}=n
then there exists a
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that

(i)

f\geq 0
on
[a,b]
,

(ii)

f
has precisely the zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
, and

(iii)

the zeros
x_{i}
of
f
have multiplicity exactly
m_{i}
.

Proof.

(a): Set
m:=m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}
.
If all
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in(a,b)
and
n=m+p
for some
p\in\mathds{N}_{0}
then the polynomial

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p}\cdot\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cccccccc}f_{0}&\,f_{1}&\dots&f_{p}&f_{p+1}&\dots&f_{p+m_{1}}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x&\,(a&\dots&a)&(x_{1}&\dots&x_{1})&\dots&x_{k})\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cccccccc}f_{0}&\,f_{1}&\dots&f_{m_{1}}&\dots&f_{m}&f_{m+1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x&\,(x_{1}&\dots&x_{1})&\dots&x_{k})&(b&\dots&b)\end{array}\right)

fulfills the requirements.
If
x_{1}=a
and/or
x_{k}=b
then include
x_{1}=a
with multiplicity
m_{1}
or
m_{1}+1
and
x_{k}=b
with multiplicity
m_{k}
or
m_{k}+1
.
Use the choice
m_{1}
or
m_{1}+1
resp.
m_{k}
or
m_{k}+1
to let
p\in 2\mathds{N}_{0}
and add
y
and
z
with
x_{k-1}<y<z<x_{k}
.
Once construct a polynomial with the zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y
with the corresponding multiplicities and add another polynomial with the zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},z
with the corresponding multiplicities to it as above.

(b): Use
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{m}
as the ET-system in (a).
∎

Chapter 6
Generating ET-Systems from T-Systems by Using Kernels

We have seen that ET- and especially ECT-systems have much nicer properties than T-systems.
Therefore, especially for technical reasons, it is desirable to smoothen a T-system into an ET-system.
Usually, a function is smoothed by convolution with e.g. the Gaussian kernel.
This procedure is also used for T-systems.

32
Kernels

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be sets and

K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathds{R}

be a bivariate function, also called
kernel
.
A family
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
on
\mathcal{Y}
can then be seen as a special case of
K
with
\mathcal{X}=\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, i.e.,
f_{i}=K(i,\,\cdot\,)
for all
i\in\mathcal{X}
.
For a kernel
K
we define the short hand notation

K\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\\
y_{0}&y_{1}&\dots&y_{n}\end{pmatrix}:=\det(K(x_{i},y_{j}))_{i,j=0}^{n}.

(49)

Definition 6.1
.

Let
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be ordered sets, and
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathds{R}
be a kernel.
The kernel
K
is called
totally positive
(
of order
k
), short (TP
k
) property, if for all
i=0,1,\dots,k
we have

K\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{i}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&\dots&y_{i}\end{pmatrix}\geq 0

for all
x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{i}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}<\dots<y_{i}
, and
(x_{l},y_{m})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
for all
l,m=1,\dots,i
.
The kernel
K
is called
strictly totally positive
(
of order
k
), short (STP
k
), if we always have

K\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{i}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&\dots&y_{i}\end{pmatrix}>0.

For more on sign regular kernels see e.g.
Kar 68
and
GM 96
.

Corollary 6.2

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 10, Exm. 3)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, let
K
be a STP
n+1
kernel with
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
,
\mathcal{Y}=[c,d]
and
K(x,\,\cdot\,)\in\mathcal{C}([c,d],\mathds{R})
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and let
x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}
in
\mathcal{X}
.

Then
\{K(x_{i},\,\cdot\,)\}_{i=0}^{k}
is a continuous T-system on
\mathcal{Y}=[c,d]
for all
k=0,\dots,n
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from
4.5
.
∎

Definition 6.3
.

Let
k\in\mathds{N}
,
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
,
\mathcal{Y}=[c,d]
, and
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathds{R}
be a kernel such that
K(x,\,\cdot\,)\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathcal{Y},\mathds{R})
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. We define

K^{*}\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{k}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&\dots&y_{k}\end{pmatrix}:=\det\begin{pmatrix}K(x_{1},\,\cdot\,)&K(x_{2},\,\cdot\,)&\dots&K(x_{k},\,\cdot\,)\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&\dots&y_{k}\end{pmatrix}^{*}

(50)

for all
x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{k}
in
\mathcal{X}
and
y_{1}\leq y_{2}\leq\dots\leq y_{k}
in
\mathcal{Y}
.

We say
K
is
extended totally positive
(
of order
k
), short ETP
k
, if for all
i=1,2,\dots,k
we have

K^{*}\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{i}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&\dots&y_{i}\end{pmatrix}>0

for all
x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{i}
in
\mathcal{X}
and
y_{1}\leq y_{2}\leq\dots\leq y_{i}
in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Corollary 6.4

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 10, Exm. 3)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, let
K
be an ETP
n+1
kernel with
\mathcal{X}=[a,b]
,
\mathcal{Y}=[c,d]
and
K(x,\,\cdot\,)\in\mathcal{C}^{n}([c,d],\mathds{R})
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and let
x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}
in
\mathcal{X}
.

Then
\{K(x_{i},\,\cdot\,)\}_{i=0}^{n}
is an ECT-system on
\mathcal{Y}=[c,d]
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from
5.3
.
∎

Example 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathds{R}
,
\mathcal{Y}=[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)
, and
K(x,y)=y^{x}
. Then
K
is ETP
k
for all
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.
\circ

Proof.

Follows immediately from
5.18
.
∎

Example 6.6

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 11, Exm. 5)
)
.

For any
\sigma>0
the Gaussian kernel

K_{\sigma}(x,y):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{x-y}{\sigma}\right)^{2}\right)\qquad\text{on\; $\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}=\mathds{R}^{2}$}

(51)

is ETP
k
for any
k\in\mathds{N}
.

The proof is adapted from
(
KS 66
, p. 11)
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that
K(x,y)=e^{-(x-y)^{2}}
is ETP
k
for all
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.

In Example
5.18
(b) we have seen that
\{e^{\alpha_{i}x}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is an ECT-system on
\mathds{R}
for all
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and all
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
in
\mathds{R}
.
Hence, by writing

f_{n}(x):=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{n}\cdot e^{-(x_{i}-x)^{2}}\qquad\text{as}\qquad f_{n}(x)=e^{-x^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\cdot e^{-x_{i}^{2}}\cdot e^{2x_{i}x}

we see that
f_{n}
has at most
n
zeros (counting multiplicities) in
\mathds{R}
if
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathds{R}
with
a_{0}^{2}+\dots+a_{n}^{2}>0
.
∎

33
The Basic Composition Formulas

The following equations (
52
) and (
54
) are the
basic composition formulas
.

Lemma 6.7

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, pp. 13–14, Exm. 8)
)

Let
K:[a,b]\times[c,d]\to\mathds{R}
and
L:[c,d]\times[e,f]\to\mathds{R}
be kernels. Let
\mu
be a
\sigma
-finite measure such that
M(x,z)
defined by

M:[a,b]\times[e,f]\to\mathds{R},\quad M(x,z):=\int_{c}^{d}K(x,y)\cdot L(y,z)~\mathrm{d}\mu(y)

exists for all
(x,z)\in[a,b]\times[e,f]
.
The following hold:

(i)

M
is a kernel.

(ii)

For all
k\in\mathds{N}
,
x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
in
[a,b]
, and
z_{1}<\dots<z_{k}
in
[e,f]
we have

M\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&\dots&x_{k}\\
z_{1}&\dots&z_{k}\end{pmatrix}=\\
\underset{c\leq y_{1}<\dots<y_{k}\leq d}{\int\;\,\dots\;\,\int}K\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&\dots&x_{k}\\
y_{1}&\dots&y_{k}\end{pmatrix}\cdot L\!\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&\dots&y_{k}\\
z_{1}&\dots&z_{k}\end{pmatrix}~\mathrm{d}\mu(y_{1})\dots\mathrm{d}\mu(y_{k}).

(52)

(iii)

If
L(y,\,\cdot\,)\in\mathcal{C}^{k-1}([e,f],\mathds{R})
for some
k\in\mathds{N}
and

\partial_{z}^{i}M(x,z):=\int_{c}^{d}K(x,y)\cdot\partial_{z}^{i}L(y,z)~\mathrm{d}\mu(y)

(53)

holds for all
i=0,\dots,k-1
then

M^{*}\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&\dots&x_{k}\\
z_{1}&\dots&z_{k}\end{pmatrix}=\\
\underset{c\leq y_{1}<\dots<y_{k}\leq d}{\int\;\,\dots\;\,\int}K\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&\dots&x_{k}\\
y_{1}&\dots&y_{k}\end{pmatrix}\cdot L^{*}\!\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&\dots&y_{k}\\
z_{1}&\dots&z_{k}\end{pmatrix}~\mathrm{d}\mu(y_{1})\dots\mathrm{d}\mu(y_{k})

(54)

for all
x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
in
[a,b]
, and
z_{1}\leq\dots\leq z_{k}
in
[e,f]
.

Proof.

(i) is clear, (ii) follows by straight forward calculations, see e.g.
(
PS 70
, p. 48, No. 68)
, and (iii) follows from (ii) with (
53
).
∎

34
Smoothing T-Systems into ET-Systems

With the Gaussian kernel from
6.6
we get from
6.7
the following smoothing result.

Corollary 6.8

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 15)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a continuous T-system on
[a,b]
.
For any
\sigma>0
let

K_{\sigma}(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{x}{\sigma}\right)^{2}\right)\qquad\text{on\; $\mathcal{X}=\mathds{R}$}

be the Gaussian kernel and define
f_{i,\sigma}:=f_{i}*K_{\sigma}
for all
i=0,\dots,n
.
Then
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}:=\{f_{i,\sigma}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is an ET-system.

Proof.

See Problem
6
.
∎

If
\mathcal{F}
is a continuous T-system on
[a,b]
then

\lim_{\sigma\searrow 0}f_{i,\sigma}(x)=f_{i}(x)

for all
x\in(a,b)
and
i=0,\dots,n
.

Corollary 6.9

If
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
in
6.8
is a T-system for all
k=0,\dots,n
then
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
is an ECT-system.

Proof.

Apply
6.8
for every
k=0,1,\dots,n
.
∎

Approximating a T-system by ET-systems with the Gaussian kernel is often used
GK 02
;
Sch 53
;
Kar 68
, see also
(
KS 66
, p. 16)
.
We will need it in the proof of
7.1
.

{prob}

Prove
6.8
from
6.7
.
