Mathematics is the tool specially suited for dealing with

abstract concepts of any kind and there is no limit to its

power in this field.

Paul Adrien Maurice Dirac
(
Dir 58
, p. viii)

Chapter 10
Non-Negative Algebraic Polynomials on
[0,\infty)
and on
\mathds{R}

We went a long way to arrive here.
But by using
8.1
and
8.3
on the interval
[0,\infty)
we can now describe all sparse algebraic strictly positive and non-negative polynomials on
[0,\infty)
and on
\mathds{R}
.

45
Sparse Algebraic Positivstellensatz on
[0,\infty)

For the sparse algebraic Positivstellensatz on
[a,b]
(
9.1
) we had a lot of freedom in the exponents
\alpha_{i}
for
a>0
.
We no longer have such a large range of freedom on
[0,\infty)
.
If we now plug
4.16
into
8.1
we get the following.

Theorem 10.1

(Sparse Algebraic Positivstellensatz on
[0,\infty)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in[0,\infty)
be real numbers with
\alpha_{0}=0<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
, and let
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{\alpha_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
on
[0,\infty)
.
Then for any
f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
with
f>0
on
[0,\infty)
and
a_{n}>0
there exists a unique decomposition

f=f_{*}+f^{*}

with
f_{*},f^{*}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
and
f_{*},f^{*}\geq 0
on
[0,\infty)
such that the following hold:

(i)

If
n=2m
then the polynomials
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
each possess
m
distinct zeros
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i=0}^{m-1}
satisfying

0=y_{0}<x_{1}<y_{1}<\dots<y_{m-1}<x_{m}<\infty.

The polynomials
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
are given by

f_{*}(x)=c_{*}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}&x^{\alpha_{2m}}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle f^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=c^{*}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&x^{\alpha_{3}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-2}}&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
x&(y_{1}&y_{1})&\dots&(y_{m-1}&y_{m-1})\end{pmatrix}

for some
c_{*},c^{*}>0
.

(ii)

If
n=2m+1
then
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
have zeros
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+1}
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
respectively which satisfy

0=x_{1}<y_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<y_{m}<x_{m+1}<\infty.

The polynomials
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
are given by

\displaystyle f_{*}(x)

\displaystyle=c_{*}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&x^{\alpha_{3}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m}}&x^{\alpha_{2m+1}}\\
x&(x_{2}&x_{2})&\dots&(x_{m+1}&x_{m+1})\end{pmatrix}

and

f^{*}(x)=c^{*}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}&x^{\alpha_{2m}}\\
x&(y_{1}&y_{1})&\dots&(y_{m}&y_{m})\end{pmatrix}

for some
c_{*},c^{*}>0
.

Proof.

We have that
\mathcal{F}
fulfills conditions (a) and (b) of
8.1
and by
4.15
we known that
\mathcal{F}
on
[0,\infty)
is also a T-system, i.e., (c) in
8.1
is fulfilled.
We can therefore apply
8.1
.

(i)
n=2m
: By
8.1
(i) the unique
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
each possess
m
distinct zeros
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i=0}^{m-1}
with
0\leq y_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<y_{m-1}<x_{m}<\infty
.
Since
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in(0,\infty)
and
\mathcal{F}
on
[x_{1}/2,\infty)
is an ET-system we immediately get the determinantal representation of
f_{*}
by
8.2
(combine
8.1
with
4.28
).
For
f^{*}
we have
y_{0}=0
and by
5.16
this is no ET-system.
Hence, we prove the representation of
f^{*}
by hand, similar as in the proof of
9.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be such that
0=y_{0}<y_{1}<y_{1}+\varepsilon<\dots<y_{m-1}<y_{m-1}+\varepsilon
holds. Then

\displaystyle g_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{-m+1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&x^{\alpha_{3}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-2}}&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
x&0&y_{1}&y_{1}+\varepsilon&\dots&y_{m-1}&y_{m-1}+\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{-m+1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
1&0&0&\dots&0\\
1&y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}&y_{1}^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&y_{1}^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
1&(y_{m-1}+\varepsilon)^{\alpha_{1}}&(y_{m-1}+\varepsilon)^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&(y_{m-1}+\varepsilon)^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\end{pmatrix}

expand by the second row

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-m+1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}&y_{1}^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&y_{1}^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
(y_{m-1}+\varepsilon)^{\alpha_{1}}&(y_{m-1}+\varepsilon)^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&(y_{m-1}+\varepsilon)^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-m+1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-2}}&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
x&y_{1}&y_{1}+\varepsilon&\dots&y_{m-1}&y_{m-1}+\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}

is non-negative on
[0,y_{1}]
and every
[y_{i}+\varepsilon,y_{i+1}]
.
Now
y_{0}=0
is removed and all
y_{i},y_{i}+\varepsilon>0
.
Hence, we can work on
[y_{1}/2,\infty)
where
\{x^{\alpha_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{2m}
is an ET-system and we can go to the limit
\varepsilon\searrow 0
as in
4.28
.
Then
8.2
proves the representation of
f^{*}
.

(ii)
n=2m+1
: Similar to the case (i) with
n=2m
.
∎

If all
\alpha_{i}\in\mathds{N}_{0}
then we can express the
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
in
10.1
also with Schur polynomials, see (
48
) in
5.17
.

We now prove a stronger version of (
16
), i.e.,
p=f^{2}+x\cdot g^{2}
for any
p\geq 0
on
[0,\infty)
.
It is sufficient to have only the sparse algebraic Positivstellensatz (
10.1
).
A previous version already appeared in
KS 53
.

Corollary 10.2

(see
(
KS 66
, p. 169, Cor. 8.1)
)

Let
p\in\mathds{R}[x]
with
p\geq 0
on
[0,\infty)
.
Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}\in[0,\infty)
be the zeros of
p
in
[0,\infty)
and let
m_{1},\dots,m_{r}\in\mathds{N}
be the corresponding algebraic multiplicities.

(i)

If
\deg p-m_{1}-\dots-m_{r}=2m
,
m\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, is even then there exist points
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}\subseteq(0,\infty)
with

0<x_{1}<y_{1}<\dots<y_{m-1}<x_{m}<\infty

and constants
a,b>0
such that

p(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(x-z_{i})^{m_{i}}\cdot\left(a\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x-x_{i})^{2}+b\cdot x\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(x-y_{i})^{2}\right).

The constant
a
is the leading coefficient of
p
.

(ii)

If
\deg p-m_{1}-\dots-m_{r}=2m+1
,
m\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, is odd then there exist points
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset(0,\infty)
with

0<x_{1}<y_{1}<\dots<x_{m}<y_{m}<\infty

and constants
a,b>0
such that

p(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(x-z_{i})^{m_{i}}\cdot\left(a\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x-x_{i})^{2}+b\cdot x\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x-y_{i})^{2}\right).

The constant
b
is the leading coefficient of
p
.

Proof.

Since
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
are the zeros of
p
in
[0,\infty)
with multiplicities
m_{1},\dots,m_{r}
we have that
p(x)=(x-z_{1})^{m_{1}}\cdots(x-z_{r})^{m_{r}}\cdot\tilde{p}(x)
with
\tilde{p}\in\mathds{R}[x]
and
\tilde{p}>0
on
[0,\infty)
. Applying
10.1
to
\tilde{p}
gives the assertion.
∎

Note, in the previous result we were able to factor out the zeros of
p
and were only left with
\tilde{p}>0
on
[0,\infty)
since we are working in
\mathds{R}[x]_{\leq\deg p}
where all monomials
1,x,\dots,x^{\deg p}
are present.
In sparse systems we are not able to factor out the zeros since we no longer know which monomials in
\tilde{p}
will appear.

Remark 10.3
.

Working in the sparse setting, i.e., in T-systems, gives us an additional information.
In (
16
) we only have
p(x)=x\cdot f^{2}+g^{2}
.
But this also includes that
f
and
g
might contain factors
((x-y_{i})^{2}+\delta_{i})
with
\delta_{i}>0
, i.e., a pair of complex conjugated zeros can be present.
In
10.2
we see that this is not necessary.
The polynomials
f
and
g
can always be chosen such that they decompose into linear factors having only real zeros.
A similar results holds on
\mathds{R}
, see
10.7
.
\circ

46
Sparse Stieltjes Moment Problem

In
Section

18
we have seen that Boas already investigated the sparse Stieltjes moment problem
Boa39a
.
However, the description was complicated and is even incomplete since Boas did not had access to
8.1
and therefore
10.1
.
We get the following complete and simple description.
It fully solves
Boa39a
.
We are not aware of a reference for the following result.

Theorem 10.4

(Sparse Stieltjes Moment Problem)

Let
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\subseteq[0,\infty)
be such that
\alpha_{0}=0<\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\dots
and let
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{\alpha_{i}}\}_{i\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

L:\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\to\mathds{R}
is a
[0,\infty)
-moment functional.

(ii)

L(p)\geq 0
for all
p\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
with
p\geq 0
.

(iii)

L(p)\geq 0
for all
p\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
with
p>0
.

(iv)

L(p)\geq 0
for all

p(x)=\begin{cases}\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}&x^{\alpha_{2m}}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix},\\
\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&x^{\alpha_{3}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-2}}&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m-1}&x_{m-1})\end{pmatrix},\\
\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&x^{\alpha_{3}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m}}&x^{\alpha_{2m+1}}\\
x&(x_{2}&x_{2})&\dots&(x_{m+1}&x_{m+1})\end{pmatrix},\ \text{and}\\
\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{\alpha_{1}}&x^{\alpha_{2}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{2m-1}}&x^{\alpha_{2m}}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix}\end{cases}

for all
m\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and
0<x_{1}<\dots<x_{m}
.

Proof.

The implications “(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii)” are clear and “(iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv)” is
10.1
.
It is therefore sufficient to prove “(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)”.

We have
\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}=(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}-(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}
, we have
1=x^{\alpha_{0}}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
, and for any
g=\sum_{i=0}^{m}a_{i}\cdot x^{\alpha_{i}}\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}
we have
\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{g(x)}{x^{\alpha_{m+1}}}=0
, i.e., there exists a
f\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}
which dominates
g
.
Hence,
\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
is an adapted space on
[0,\infty)
and the assertion follows from the
2.9
.
∎

In the previous result we did needed
0=\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\dots
. We did not needed
\alpha_{i}\to\infty
.
Hence,
10.4
also includes the case
\sup_{i\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\alpha_{i}<\infty
.

10.4
also holds with
\alpha_{0}>0
since we can factor out
x^{\alpha_{0}}
and therefore determine
x^{\alpha_{0}}~\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
instead of
\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
.

47
Sparse Algebraic Nichtnegativstellensatz on
[0,\infty)

For
\{1,x,x^{3}\}
we have seen in
5.16
that this is not an ET-systen on
[0,\infty)
, or on any other
[0,b]
.
If we remove the point
x=0
and work on
(0,\infty)
then it is an ET-system and even an ECT-system (
5.18
).
For a Nichtnegativstellensatz we therefore have to exclude zeros at
x=0
in a sparse polynomial
p\geq 0
.

Theorem 10.5

(Sparse Algebraic Nichtnegativstellensatz on
[0,\infty)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in[0,\infty)
be real numbers with
\alpha_{0}=0<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
, and let
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{\alpha_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
.
Let
f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{\alpha_{i}}\geq 0
on
[0,\infty)
with
a_{n}>0
and
f(0)=a_{0}>0
.
Then there exist points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1}\in[0,\infty)
(not necessarily distinct) which include the zeros of
f
with multiplicities and there exist constants
c_{*},c^{*}\in\mathds{R}
such that

f=f_{*}+f^{*}

with
f_{*},f^{*}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
,
f_{*},f^{*}\geq 0
on
[0,\infty)
, and the polynomials
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
are given by

f_{*}(x)=c_{*}\cdot\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}1&\ x^{\alpha_{1}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{n}}\\
x&\ x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{array}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad f^{*}(x)=c_{*}\cdot\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}1&\ x^{\alpha_{1}}&\dots&x^{\alpha_{n-1}}\\
x&\ y_{1}&\dots&y_{n-1}\end{array}\right)

for all
x\in[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

See Problem
10
.
∎

Remark 10.6
.

Note, if
f(0)=a_{0}=0
in
10.5
then

f(x)=a_{i}x^{\alpha_{i}}+a_{i+1}x^{\alpha_{i+1}}+\dots+a_{n}x^{\alpha_{n}}=x^{\alpha_{i}}\cdot(\underbrace{a_{i}+a_{i+1}x^{\alpha_{i+1}-\alpha_{i}}+\dots+a_{n}x^{\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{i}}}_{=:\tilde{f}(x)})

where
a_{i}
is the first non-zero coefficient and it fulfills
a_{i}>0
since
f\geq 0
.
Then apply
10.5
to
\tilde{f}
to get
\tilde{f}=\tilde{f}_{*}+\tilde{f}^{*}
and hence
f=x^{\alpha_{i}}\cdot(\tilde{f}_{*}+\tilde{f}^{*})
.

\circ

48
Algebraic Positiv- and Nichtnegativstellensatz on
\mathds{R}

Since we treat
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
we need only
8.4
on
\mathds{R}
but not
8.5
on
\mathds{R}
as we will see in the next result.

Theorem 10.7

(Algebraic Positiv- and Nichtnegativstellensatz on
\mathds{R}
, see
KS 53
or e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 198, Cor. 8.1)
)

Let
p\in\mathds{R}[x]
with
p\geq 0
on
\mathds{R}
and let
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}\in\mathds{R}
be the zeros of
p
with algebraic multiplicities
m_{1},\dots,m_{r}\in 2\mathds{N}
.
Then there exist pairwise distinct points
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m},\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}\subseteq\mathds{R}
with
2m=\deg p-m_{1}-\dots-m_{r}
and

-\infty<x_{1}<y_{1}<\dots<y_{m-1}<x_{m}<\infty

as well as constants
a,b>0
such that

p(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(x-z_{i})^{m_{i}}\cdot\left(a\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x-x_{i})^{2}+b\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(x-y_{i})^{2}\right).

(65)

The constant
a
is the leading coefficient of
p
.

Proof.

We have
p(x)=(x-z_{1})^{m_{1}}\cdots(x-z_{r})^{m_{r}}\cdot\tilde{p}(x)
for some
\tilde{p}\in\mathds{R}[x]
with
\tilde{p}>0
on
\mathds{R}
. Applying
8.4
to
\tilde{p}
gives the assertion.
∎

Like in the case on
[0,\infty)
in
10.2
a factorization

p(x)=(x-z_{1})^{m_{1}}\cdots(x-z_{r})^{m_{r}}\cdot\tilde{p}(x)

is not possible in T-systems or sparse algebraic systems on
\mathds{R}
.
But since we are working in
\mathds{R}[x]_{\leq\deg p}
all monomials
1,x,\dots,x^{\deg p}
are present.

Remark 10.8
.

Similar to
10.3
we see that
10.7
gives a stronger version of (
14
), i.e.,
p=f^{2}+g^{2}
.
By applying only the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra
f
and
g
might contain pairs of complex conjugated zeros, see e.g.
(
Mar 08
, Prop. 1.2.1)
.
But by working in the T-system framework of
8.4
on
\mathds{R}
we see that
f
and
g
can be chosen to have only real zeros.
\circ

Problems

{prob}

Use
8.3
to prove
10.5
.

{prob}

Show that
a
in (
65
) in
10.7
is the leading coefficient of
p
.

Part V
Applications of T-Systems

\motto

Long is the way and hard, that out of Hell leads up to light.

John Milton: Paradise Lost

Chapter 11
Moment Problems for continuous T-Systems on
[a,b]

In this chapter we demonstrate how e.g.
7.3
for general T-systems on
[a,b]
can be used to prove moment problems which do not live on the algebraic polynomials
\mathds{R}[x]
.

49
General Moment Problems for continuous T-Systems on
[a,b]

For T-system
\mathcal{F}
on
[a,b]

7.3
describes all polynomials
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
with
f>0
.

Theorem 11.1

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
, let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be a continuous T-system on
[a,b]
with
a<b
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

L:\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\to\mathds{R}
is an
[a,b]
-moment functional.

(ii)

L(f)\geq 0
for all
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that

(a)

f\geq 0
on
[a,b]
and

(b)

the zero set of
f
has index
n
.

Proof.

The implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is clear since
f\geq 0
.
It is therefore sufficient to prove (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i).

Since
\mathcal{F}
is a continuous T-system there exists a polynomial
e\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
with
e>0
on
[a,b]
.
Since
[a,b]
is compact,
\mathcal{F}
is continuous and finite dimensional, and there exists a
e>0
we have that the moment cone
((\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+})^{*}
is closed.
Therefore, to show that
L
is a moment functional it is sufficient to show that
L(f)\geq 0
for all
f\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}
.

By
7.3
there are
e_{*},e^{*}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
with
e_{*},e^{*}\geq 0
and the zero sets of
e_{*}
and of
e^{*}
have index
n
.
Hence,
L(e)=L(e_{*})+L(e^{*})\geq 0
.

Let
f\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Then
f_{\varepsilon}=f+\varepsilon\cdot e>0
on
[a,b]
, i.e., by
7.3
there exist
(f_{\varepsilon})_{*},(f_{\varepsilon})^{*}\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}
each with zero sets of index
n
.
Assumption (ii) then implies
L(f+\varepsilon\cdot e)=L((f_{\varepsilon})_{*})+L((f_{\varepsilon})^{*})\geq 0
for all
\varepsilon>0
, i.e.,
L(f)\geq 0
.
That proves the assertion.
∎

Note, that a continuous T-system on
[a,b]
is always an adapted space.
Additionally, the use of
2.9
is not necessary since we only need to check in this case
L\in((\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+})^{*}
since the moment cone is
((\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+})^{*}
and hence it is closed.

If in the previous theorem we additionally have that
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system then we can write down
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
explicitly in the similar way as in
9.6
.

Theorem 11.2

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
, let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be an ET-system on
[a,b]
with
a<b
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

L:\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\to\mathds{R}
is a moment functional.

(ii)

L(f)\geq 0
holds for all

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix}\\
-\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&\dots&f_{2m-2}&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}\\
x&a&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m-1}&x_{m-1})&b\end{pmatrix}\end{cases}

if

n=2m

and

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}-\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&\dots&f_{2m}&f_{2m+1}\\
x&a&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix}\\
\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}&f_{2m+1}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})&b\end{pmatrix}\end{cases}

if

n=2m+1

and all
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
with
a<x_{1}<\dots<x_{m}<b
.

Proof.

Follows from
11.1
with
5.3
.
∎

50
A Non-Polynomial Example

In
4.18
we have seen that

\mathcal{F}=\left\{\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{0}},\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{1}},\dots,\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{n}}\right\}

with
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
reals is a continuous T-system on any
[a,b]
with
-\alpha_{0}<a<b
, see Problem
4
for the proof.
But in the proof of
4.18
we actually showed that this
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system since we multiplied with
(x+\alpha_{0})\cdots(x+\alpha_{n})
which has no zeros on
[a,b]
and hence the multiplicities of the zeros do not change.
Multiplicity restriction from the fundamental theorem of algebra then shows that
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system.

Corollary 11.3

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
be reals. Then

\mathcal{F}=\left\{\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{0}},\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{1}},\dots,\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{n}}\right\}

is an ET-system on any
[a,b]
with
-\alpha_{0}<a<b
.

From
11.2
and
11.3
we therefore get the following.

Corollary 11.4

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
, let
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}
be reals, and let

\mathcal{F}=\left\{f_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{0}},f_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{1}},\dots,f_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{x+\alpha_{n}}\right\}

on
[a,b]
with
-\alpha_{0}<a<b
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

L:\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\to\mathds{R}
is a
[a,b]
-moment functional.

(ii)

L(f)\geq 0
holds for all

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix}\\
-\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&\dots&f_{2m-2}&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}\\
x&a&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m-1}&x_{m-1})&b\end{pmatrix}\end{cases}

if

n=2m

and

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}-\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&\dots&f_{2m}&f_{2m+1}\\
x&a&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})\end{pmatrix}\\
\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&\dots&f_{2m-1}&f_{2m}&f_{2m+1}\\
x&(x_{1}&x_{1})&\dots&(x_{m}&x_{m})&b\end{pmatrix}\end{cases}

if

n=2m+1

and all
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
with
a<x_{1}<\dots<x_{m}<b
.

In a similar way many other T-system moment problems can be proven from
11.1
.

\motto

The rest is silence.

William Shakespeare: Hamlet (Act 5, Scene 2)

Chapter 12
Polynomials of Best Approximation and Optimization over Linear Functionals

This last chapter is devoted to best approximation polynomials and optimization over linear functionals.

We started in
Chapter

1
with moments and moment functionals, went to the theory of T-systems in
Part

II
, proved Karlin’s Theorems in
Part

III
, and applied them to algebraic polynomials in
Part

IV
.
Now we finish our lecture by closing the circle.
We apply the previous results to best approximation in
Section

51
and to optimization over linear (moment) functionals in
Section

52
.

51
Polynomials of Best Approximation

A classical question is:

How to approximate a given function
f\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
in the
\sup
-norm by a finite linear combination
\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}
of some given
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
?

Definition 12.1
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, let
f,f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
, and let
\mathcal{F}:=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
.
The polynomial
\underline{f}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
which solves

\min_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}}\left\|\;f-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\;\right\|_{\infty}

(66)

is called the
polynomial of best approximation
.

Approximations (
66
) with the
\sup
-norm are called
Tchebycheff approximations
.

The connection between polynomials of best approximation and T-systems is revealed in the following result.

Theorem 12.2

(see
Haa 18
,
Ber 26
; or e.g.
(
Ach 56
, p. 74, §48)
,
(
KS 66
, p. 280, Thm. 1.1)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, let
a,b\in\mathds{R}
with
a<b
, and let
\mathcal{F}:=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be a family of continuous functions.
The following hold:

(i)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

The polynomial minimizing

\min_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}}\left\|\;f-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\;\right\|_{\infty}

(67)

is uniquely determined for every
f\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
.

(b)

The family
\mathcal{F}
is a continuous T-system on
[a,b]
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system then for each
f\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
the unique polynomial

\underline{f}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\underline{a}_{i}f_{i}

minimizing (
67
) is characterized by the property that there exist
n+2
points

a\leq\quad x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{n+2}\quad\leq b

such that

(-1)^{i}\cdot\delta\cdot(f(x_{i})-\underline{f}(x_{i}))=\max_{a\leq x\leq b}\left|\,f(x)-\underline{f}(x)\,\right|

holds for all
i=1,2,\dots,n+2
with
\delta=+1
or
-1
.

Statement (i) of the previous theorem is essentially due to A. Haar
Haa 18
.
The following proof significantly differs from Haar’s proof and it is more general.
It is taken from
(
KS 66
, pp. 284–286)
, see also
(
Ach 56
, pp. 75–76)
.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b): We prove
\neg
(b)
\Rightarrow\neg
(a).

Assume
\mathcal{F}
is not a T-system. There exist
n+1
distinct points
a\leq x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}\leq b
such that

\det\left(f_{i}(x_{j})\right)_{i,j=0}^{n}=0.

(68)

Hence, there exist real coefficients
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
with
\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}^{2}>0
with
\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}f_{j}(x_{i})=0
for all
j=0,\dots,n
.
That implies

\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}p(x_{i})=0

(69)

for all
p\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
.

The relation (
68
) also implies the existence of a non-trivial polynomial
\tilde{p}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}f_{i}\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
which vanishes at the points
x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be such that
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and

g(x_{i})=\frac{c_{i}}{|c_{i}|}

for all
i=0,1,\dots,n
with
c_{i}\neq 0
.

Let
\lambda>0
be such that
\|\lambda\tilde{p}\|_{\infty}<1
then
f:=g\cdot(1-|\lambda\tilde{p}|)
has the same signs at the points
x_{i}
with
c_{i}\neq 0
as
g
.

We will now construct an infinite number of polynomials of the same minimum deviation from
f
.

If

\left\|\;f-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\;\right\|_{\infty}<1

for some
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
then

-1<g(x_{j})\cdot(1-|\lambda\tilde{p}(x_{j})|)-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}(x_{j})<1

for all
j=0,1,\dots,n
which reduces to

-1<g(x_{j})-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}(x_{j})<1

for all
j=0,1,\dots,n
.
Hence, if
c_{j}\neq 0
the value of
\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}(x_{j})
has the sign of the
c_{j}
so that
\sum_{j=0}^{n}c_{j}\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}(x_{j})\neq 0
which contradicts (
69
).
Therefore,

\left\|\;f-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\;\right\|_{\infty}\geq 1.

If now
|\delta|<1
then

\displaystyle|f(x)-\delta\lambda\tilde{p}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|f(x)|+|\delta\lambda\tilde{p}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|g(x)|\cdot(1-|\lambda\tilde{p}(x))+|\delta\lambda\tilde{p}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq 1-(1-|\delta|)\cdot|\lambda\tilde{p}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq 1

so that
\delta\lambda\tilde{p}
minimizes the distance to
f
independent of
\delta\in(-1,1)
.
Hence, we proved
\neg
(a).

We now prove (ii) which will also establish (b)
\Rightarrow
(a).
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a T-system.
At least one minimal polynomial exists since
\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
is finite dimensional.
Assume
g=\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}f_{i}
fulfills

\|f-g\|_{\infty}=m=\min_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}}\left\|\;f-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\;\right\|_{\infty}

and
f-g
takes on the values
\pm m
alternatively at only
k\leq n+1
points.
We suppose for definiteness that
f-g
assumes the values
+m
before it takes the value
-m
.
In this case there exist
k-1
points

a\leq\quad y_{1}<\dots<y_{k-1}\quad\leq b

such that

f(y_{i})-g(y_{i})=0

for all
i=1,2,\dots,k-1
and for some
d>0
we have

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\;\geq\;f-g\;\geq\;-m+d

\displaystyle\text{on}\ [a,y_{1}]\cup[y_{2},y_{3}]\cup\dots

\displaystyle m-d

\displaystyle\;\geq\;f-g\;\geq\;-m

\displaystyle\text{on}\ [y_{1},y_{2}]\cup[y_{3},y_{4}]\cup.

By
4.30
and
4.27
there exists a polynomial
h
whose
only
zeros on the
open
interval
(a,b)
are the nodal zeros
y_{1},\dots,y_{k-1}
and additionally
h\leq 0
on
[a,y_{1}]
.
Let
\delta>0
be such that
|\delta h|\leq d/2
then

|f-g+\delta h|<m

(70)

on
(a,b)
.

Equality in (
70
) is possible at the end point
a
only if
f(a)-g(a)=m
and
h(a)=0
and at
b
only if
|f(a)-g(b)|=m
and
h(b)=0
.
To repair the situation at the points
a
and
b
let
\tilde{h}
be such that
\tilde{h}\cdot(f-g)>0
at
a
and
b
.
Then for sufficient small
\eta
we have

|f-g+\delta h-\eta\tilde{h}|<m

on
[a,b]
.
Hence, by continuity on the compact interval
[a,b]
we have

\min_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}}\left\|\;f-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\;\right\|_{\infty}<m

contradicting the fact that
m
is the minimum deviation.
That proves (ii) including uniqueness in (i).
∎

In the previous theorem we have seen the close connection between the best approximation polynomials from the minimum problem (
67
) and T-systems.
The next result shows that the connection is even closer, i.e., the solution of (
67
) is connected to the
7.4
.

Theorem 12.3

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 283, Thm. 2.1)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and let
f_{0},\dots,f_{n},f\in\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathds{R})
be such that
\{f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\}
and
\{f_{0},\dots,f_{n},f\}
are continuous T-systems on
[a,b]
with
a<b
.
Let

f^{*}=c\cdot f+\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}\cdot f_{i}

be the
f^{*}
from the
7.4
with
g_{1}=-1
and
g_{2}=1
, i.e.,
f^{*}
is uniquely characterized by the following conditions:

(a)

-1\leq f^{*}\leq 1
on
[a,b]
, and

(b)

there exist
n+2
points
x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{n+2}
in
[a,b]
such that

f^{*}(x_{i})=(-1)^{n+1-i}

for all
i=1,\dots,n+2
.

Then
c\neq 0
and the polynomial

\underline{f}:=-\frac{1}{c}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}f_{i}

is the unique minimizer of

d=\min_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}}\left\|\;f-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}\;\right\|_{\infty}

and the minimum deviation is
d=|c|^{-1}
.

The proof is taken from
(
KS 66
, pp. 283–284)
.

Proof.

The coefficient
c
can not be zero.
Otherwise the polynomial
\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}f_{i}
vanishes at
n+1
points in the T-system
\{f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\}
by (b) and would therefore be equal to zero by
4.5
.

From (a) we get

\left\|\;f-\left(-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}f_{i}\right)\;\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{|d|}.

Since
\underline{f}
fulfills (b) we get from
12.2
(ii) uniqueness of
\underline{f}
and
d=|c|^{-1}
.
∎

Finding approximations is also done with respect to the
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-norms

\min_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}}\int\left|\;f(x)-\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}f_{i}(x)\;\right|^{p}~\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

(71)

with a fixed measure
\mu
and
p\geq 1
.
For
p=2
this leads to the well-studied
orthogonal polynomials
, a special branch of the theory of moments.

For
p=1
in (
71
) this also is connected to T-systems.
D. Jackson
Jac 24
showed that if
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\}
is a T-system then the best approximation of (
71
) is unique, see also
(
Ach 56
, p. 77)
.

52
Optimization over Linear Functionals

In optimization one often encounters the problem of having only a linear functional
L:\mathcal{V}\to\mathds{R}
, e.g. a moment functional, and one wants to minimize
L(f)
over
\mathcal{V}_{+}
.
By removing the dependency on the scaling of
f
we get the following result.

Theorem 12.4

(see e.g.
(
KS 66
, p. 312, Thm. 9.1)
)

Let
n\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be an ET-system on
[a,b]
with
a<b
, and let
L,S:\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\to\mathds{R}
be two linear functionals such that
S
is strictly positive on
(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}
, i.e.,
S(f)>0
for all
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}\setminus\{0\}
with
f\geq 0
.
Then

\min_{f\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{L(f)}{S(f)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\max_{f\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{L(f)}{S(f)}

(72)

are attained at non-negative polynomials possessing
n
zeros counting multiplicities.

The proof is taken from
(
KS 66
, p. 312)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
is finite dimensional the values in (
72
) are attained.

It is sufficient to prove the statement for the maximum.
But maximizing
\frac{L(f)}{S(f)}
over
(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
is equivalent to maximize
L(f)
over
f\in(\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
with
S(f)=1
.

Let
f\geq 0
be such that
S(f)=1
and suppose
f
has at most
n-1
zeros counting multiplicities.
Then by
7.6
there is a unique decomposition
f=f_{*}+f^{*}
where
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
differ, are non-negative, and both have
n
zeros counting multiplicities.
Set
\alpha:=S(f_{*})
and
\beta:=S(f^{*})
.
Then
\alpha,\beta>0
since
S
is strictly positive and
\alpha+\beta=S(f_{*})+S(f^{*})=S(f)=1
.
Then

f=\alpha\cdot\frac{f_{*}}{\alpha}+\beta\cdot\frac{f^{*}}{\beta}

and by linearity

L(f)\leq\max\left(\frac{L(f_{*})}{\alpha},\frac{L(f^{*})}{\beta}\right)

which proves the statement.
∎

More results on best approximation and optimization over linear functionals can already be found in
Ber 26
,
Ach 56
, and
KS 66
.
Let alone the enormous literature after that.

Problems of

{sol}

prob:determinacy
The
0.3
states that for a compact set
K\subset\mathds{R}^{n}
the polynomials
\mathds{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
are dense in
\mathcal{C}(K,\mathds{R})
with respect to the
\sup
-norm.
Let
A\in\mathfrak{B}(K)
be a Borel measurable set, let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
be two representing measures of
L
.
Set
A_{\delta}:=(A+B_{\delta}(0))\cap K
for all
\delta>0
.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
such that
\mu_{1}(A_{\delta}\setminus A),\mu_{2}(A_{\delta}\setminus A)<\varepsilon
.

By
0.2
there exists a
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{C}(K,[0,1])
such that

\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)=\begin{cases}1&\text{for}\ x\in A\\
0&\text{for}\ x\in K\setminus A_{\varepsilon}\end{cases}

and since
\mathds{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
is dense in
\mathcal{C}(K,\mathds{R})
there exists a family of polynomials
(p_{i}^{\varepsilon})_{i\in\mathds{N}}\subseteq\mathds{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
such that

\|p_{i}^{\varepsilon}-\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{i\to\infty}0\quad\text{and hence}\quad\int_{K}p_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{j}(x)\xrightarrow{i\to\infty}\int_{K}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{j}(x)

for
j=1,2
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(A)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\int_{K}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{K}p_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\lim_{i\to\infty}L(p_{i}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{K}p_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\int_{K}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x)\qquad=\mu_{2}(A).

Since
A\in\mathfrak{B}(K)
was arbitrary we have
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
, i.e.,
L
has a unique representing measure and is therefore determinate.

{sol}

prob:convexML

Proof of
1.3

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(L)
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
. Then

\displaystyle\int p(x)~\mathrm{d}[\lambda\mu_{1}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{2}](x)

\displaystyle=\lambda\int p(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)+(1-\lambda)\int p(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\lambda L(p)+(1-\lambda)L(p)

\displaystyle=L(p)

and hence
\lambda\mu_{1}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(L)
which proves convexity.

{sol}

prob:indeter

Proof of
1.10

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}(L)
with
\mu_{0}\neq\mu_{1}
, i.e., there exists a
A\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mu_{0}(A)\neq\mu_{1}(A)
and without loss of generality we have
\mu_{0}(A)<\mu_{1}(A)
.
Hence, for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
we set
\mu_{\lambda}:=\lambda\mu_{1}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{0}
and we have

\mu_{\lambda_{0}}(A)<\mu_{\lambda_{1}}(A)

for all
0\leq\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq 1
which proves that
\mu_{\lambda_{0}}\neq\mu_{\lambda_{1}}
for all
\lambda_{0}\neq\lambda_{1}
.
Hence, we have at least
|[0,1]|=|\mathds{R}|
many representing measures for
L
.

prob:linearCone

Proof of
2.1

The proof is taken from
(
Cho 69
, Vol. 2, p. 268)
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): If
F+C
is a vector space then
-(F+C)=(F+C)
and
-(F+C)=F-C
since
-F=F
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii): If
x\in F+C
, i.e.,
x=y^{\prime}+z
for some
y^{\prime}\in F
and
z\in C
, then
x\geq y^{\prime}
.
Similarly, if
x=y-w
then
y\geq x
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i): First note that
F+C
is a convex cone. So if suffices to show that
F+C=-(F+C)
, i.e.,
F+C=F-C
.
But if
x\in F+C
and
x\leq y
then
x=y-z
for some
z\in C
, or
x\in F-C
.
Similarly, if
x\in F-C
and
x=y^{\prime}+w
for some
w\in C
then
x\in F+C
.

.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Take
h_{\varepsilon}=\eta_{\varepsilon}\cdot g\in\mathcal{C}_{c}(\mathcal{X},\mathds{R})
.

prob:compactAdapted
Since
\mathcal{X}
is compact for every
f\in E
we have
m_{f}:=\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)>-\infty
and
M_{f}:=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)<\infty
, especially for
f=e>0
we have
m_{e}>0
. Then for every
f
there exists a
d_{f}>0
such that
f=(f+d_{f}e)-d_{f}e
such that
f+d_{f}e,d_{f}e\in E_{+}
and hence
E=E_{+}-E_{+}
proving (i) in
2.7
.

Since
e>0
we also have (ii) in
2.7
.

For (iii) in
2.7
it is sufficient to note that
\mathcal{X}
is compact, i.e., for every
g
there is a
c_{g}>0
such that
g\leq c_{g}e
.

prob:adaptedPolynomials
Let
E=\mathds{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
on
\mathcal{X}
.
Then (i)
E=E_{+}-E_{+}
follows immediately from the fact that for every
f\in E
there is a
g\in E_{+}
such that
f=f+g-g
with
f+g\in E_{+}
.

For (ii) we take
f=1>0
on
\mathcal{X}
.

For (iii) take the
g
from (i).

prob:adaptedPolynomials2
Since
E
is finite dimensional we can equip it with a norm, e.g. the
l^{2}
-norm in the coefficients of
f
.
Assume
\mathcal{X}
is not compact then there exists an unbounded sequence
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
and a
f\in E
with
\|f\|\leq 1
such that
(f(x_{i}))_{i\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
grows faster than any other
(g(x_{i}))_{i\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
.
Hence,
f
can not be dominated by any
g
.

E

f\in E_{+}

f>0
we have that
\min_{x\in K}f(x)>0
and hence there exists a
c>0
such that
cf>g
on
K
and hence on all
\mathcal{X}
.

prob:bernstein

Proof of
3.6

We have that (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) is clear since
x^{k}\cdot(1-x)^{l}>0
on
(0,1)
and at least one
c_{k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}>0
.

\Rightarrow
(ii).

with
f>0
on
(0,1)
then we can write
f
as

f(x)=x^{p}\cdot(1-x)^{q}\cdot\tilde{f}(x)

with

on

p,q\in\mathds{N}_{0}

.

3.5
(ii) to

prob:boundaryCone

Proof of
3.9

Since the moment cone
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}
and the hyperplane
H
are convex we have that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}\cap H
is a convex cone, i.e., it is a moment cone and there exists a family
\mathcal{G}\subsetneq\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
of
m<n
elements which spans
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}\cap H
.
It is sufficient to show that
\mathcal{G}
lives on
(\mathcal{Y},\mathfrak{A}|_{\mathcal{Y}})
for some
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
.

For the hyperplane
H
there exists a function
h\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that
L_{s}(h)\geq 0
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Note, that
\mathcal{N}=\cap_{k\in\mathds{N}}\{x\in\mathcal{X}\,|\,f_{1}(x)^{2}+\dots+f_{n}(x)^{2}\geq k\}
has measure zero for any representing measure
\mu_{s}
on
\mathcal{X}
of a moment sequence
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}
since the moments are finite, i.e., the
f_{i}
are
\mu_{s}
-integrable.
Without loss of generality we can therefore work on
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N}
.
Hence, all
\delta_{x}
with
x\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N}
are moment measures and
L_{s}(h)\geq 0
implies
h\geq 0
on
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N}
.

Then
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}\cap H\Leftrightarrow L_{s}(h)=0
implies that all representing measures
\mu
of all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}\cap H
have the support in
\mathcal{Y}:=\{x\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N}\,|\,h(x)=0\}
.

{sol}

prob:richterFromRosen
Let
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
be measurable functions on
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{A})
which are not necessarily bounded.
Set

I:=\bigcap_{k\in\mathds{N}}\{x\in\mathcal{X}\,|\,|f_{i}(x)|>k\ \text{for all}\ i=1,\dots,n\}.

Then
I
is measurable.
Let
s
be a moment sequence with representing measure
\mu
.
Since all
f_{i}
are
\mu
-measurable we have
\mu(I)=0
.
Therefore, by working on
\mathcal{X}\setminus I
we can assume without loss of generality that
|f_{i}(x)|<\infty
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Define
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\}
with
g_{i}:=\frac{g_{i}}{f}
and
f:=1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}^{2}
.

At first we note that from

\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{i}(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}g_{i}(x)\cdot f(x)~\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathcal{X}}g_{i}(x)~\mathrm{d}\nu(x),

(73)

we have that every sequence
s=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
is a moment sequence with respect to
\mathcal{G}
if and only if it is moment sequence with respect to
\mathcal{F}
.

Since all
g_{i}
are bounded we have by Rosenbloom’s Theorem that there is a
k
-atomic representing measure
\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}\cdot\delta_{x_{i}}
which represents the moment sequence
s
.
Then by (
73
) we find that
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})^{-1}\cdot\delta_{x_{i}}
is a representing measure of
s
with respect to
\mathcal{F}
which proves the statement.

prob:restriction

Proof of
4.3

Let
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
. Then
f
has at most
n
zeros in
\mathcal{X}
and hence
f|_{\mathcal{Y}}
has at most
n
zeros in
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{X}
.
Since for any
g\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{G}
there is a
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{F}
such that
g=f|_{\mathcal{Y}}
we have the assertion.

prob:transf

Proof of
4.8

Let
w_{0},\dots,w_{n}\in\mathcal{W}
be pairwise distinct.
Since
g
is injective we have that also
g(w_{0}),\dots,g(w_{n})\in\mathcal{X}
are pairwise distinct.
Hence,

\det\begin{pmatrix}g_{0}&g_{1}&\dots&g_{n}\\
w_{0}&w_{1}&\dots&w_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
g(w_{0})&g(w_{1})&\dots&g(w_{n})\end{pmatrix}\neq 0

and the statement follows from
4.5
.

and the statement follows from
4.5

prob:4.1

Proof of
4.10

(i)

Assume
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
are linearly dependent, i.e., there are
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathds{R}
not all zero such that
a_{0}f_{0}+\dots+a_{n}f_{n}
is the zero polynomial. Hence,
f
has at least
n+1
zeros. But since
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system this is a contradiction.

(ii)

Let
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
be
n+1
pairwise distinct points. Then by
4.4
we have

\begin{pmatrix}f(x_{0})\\
\vdots\\
f(x_{n})\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}}_{=:M}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}\\
\vdots\\
a_{n}\end{pmatrix}

and since
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system we have that
M
has full rank by
4.5
. Hence, the coefficients
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}
are unique.

prob:fraction

Proof of
4.18

Set
f_{i}(x):=(x+\alpha_{i})^{-1}
and
g(x)=(x+\alpha_{0})\cdots(x+\alpha_{n})
.
Then
g>0
on
[a,b]
since
-\alpha_{0}<a<b
.
Hence,
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system on
[a,b]
if and only if
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{i}:=g\cdot f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system on
[a,b]
by
4.9
.

We have
g_{i}(x)=(x+\alpha_{0})\cdots(x+\alpha_{i-1})\cdot(x+\alpha_{i+1})\cdots(x+\alpha_{n})
and
\deg g_{i}=n
.
It is now sufficient to show that
\mathcal{G}
is a T-system on
\mathds{R}
by
4.3
since then it will also be a T-system on
[a,b]
.

Since
g_{i}(\alpha_{j})=0
for all
i\neq j
we have that the
g_{i}
are linearly independent.
Hence,
\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{G}=\mathds{R}[x]_{\leq n}
.
But since
\{x^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system so is
\mathcal{G}
since every non-trivial
f\in\mathrm{lin}\,\mathcal{G}=\mathds{R}[x]_{\leq n}
has at most
n
zeros.

In summary, we have that
\{x^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system on
\mathds{R}

\Rightarrow

\mathcal{G}
on
\mathds{R}
is a T-system
\Rightarrow

\mathcal{G}
on
[a,b]
is a T-system
\Rightarrow

\mathcal{F}
on
[a,b]
is a T-system.

prob:4.2
To the points
x_{0},\dots,x_{k+l}\in[a,b]
add pairwise distinct points
x_{k+l+1},\dots,x_{n}\in[a,b]\setminus\{x_{0},\dots,x_{k+l}
. Then the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x_{0})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{0})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{n})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{n})\end{pmatrix}

(74)

has full rank since
\mathcal{F}
is a T-system, i.e., every vector, especially

(m,\dots,m,-m,\dots,-m,0,\dots,0,*,\dots,*)^{T}\in\mathds{R}^{n+1}

is in its image.
But the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x_{1})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{1})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{0}(x_{k+l})&\dots&f_{n}(x_{k+l})\end{pmatrix}

in (
29
) only contains the first
k+l
rows of (
74
), i.e., (
29
) has at least one solution.

{sol}

prob:etMultiplication

Proof of
5.7

Set
g_{i}:=g\cdot f_{i}
.
Then we have to check that

\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{k})(x)=\det\begin{pmatrix}g_{0}(x)&g_{1}(x)&\dots&g_{n}(x)\\
g_{0}^{\prime}(x)&g_{1}^{\prime}(x)&\dots&g_{n}^{\prime}(x)\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
g_{0}^{(n)}(x)&g_{1}^{(n)}(x)&\dots&g_{n}^{(n)}(x)\end{pmatrix}\neq 0

holds for all
x\in[a,b]
.
Since
g_{i}=g\cdot f_{i}
we apply the product rule and get

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{k})(x)

\displaystyle=g^{2}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x)&f_{1}(x)&\dots&f_{n}(x)\\
f_{0}^{\prime}(x)&f_{1}^{\prime}(x)&\dots&f_{n}^{\prime}(x)\\
g_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)&g_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x)&\dots&g_{n}^{\prime\prime}(x)\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
g_{0}^{(n)}(x)&g_{1}^{(n)}(x)&\dots&g_{n}^{(n)}(x)\end{pmatrix}

since in the first line we factored out
g
and then subtracted
g^{\prime}
-times the first line from the second, and factored out
g
from the remaining second line.
For the second derivatives in the third line we have

(g\cdot f_{i})^{\prime\prime}=g^{\prime\prime}\cdot f_{i}+2g^{\prime}\cdot f_{i}^{\prime}+g\cdot f_{i}^{\prime\prime}

and hence subtracting
g^{\prime\prime}
-times the first row,
2g^{\prime}
-times the second row, and finally factoring out
g
from the remaining third row we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{k})(x)

\displaystyle=g^{3}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x)&f_{1}(x)&\dots&f_{n}(x)\\
f_{0}^{\prime}(x)&f_{1}^{\prime}(x)&\dots&f_{n}^{\prime}(x)\\
f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)&f_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x)&\dots&f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(x)\\
g_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)&g_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)&\dots&g_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
g_{0}^{(n)}(x)&g_{1}^{(n)}(x)&\dots&g_{n}^{(n)}(x)\end{pmatrix}.

Proceeding in this manner we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{k})(x)

\displaystyle=g^{n+1}\cdot\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})(x)\neq 0

for all
x\in[a,b]
which proves the statement.

{sol}

prob:wronsTrans

Proof of
5.8

We proceed similar to Problem/Solution
5
but now with the rule of differentiation for
f_{i}\circ g
.
We have

(f_{i}\circ g)^{\prime}=g^{\prime}\cdot(f_{i}^{\prime}\circ g)

and hence

\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{n})=g^{\prime}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}\circ g&\dots&f_{n}\circ g\\
f_{0}^{\prime}\circ g&\dots&f_{n}^{\prime}\circ g\\
(f_{0}\circ g)^{\prime\prime}&\dots&(f_{n}\circ g)^{\prime\prime}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
(f_{0}\cdot g)^{(n)}&\dots&(f_{n}\circ g)^{(n)}\end{pmatrix}

by factoring out
g^{\prime}
from the second row.
Then we have

\displaystyle(f_{i}\circ g)^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(g^{\prime}\cdot(f_{i}^{\prime}\circ g))^{\prime}=g^{\prime\prime}\cdot(f_{i}^{\prime}\circ g)+(g^{\prime})^{2}\cdot(f_{i}^{\prime\prime}\circ g),

i.e., we subtract
g^{\prime\prime}
-times the second row and factor out
(g^{\prime})^{2}
to get

\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{n})=(g^{\prime})^{3}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}\circ g&\dots&f_{n}\circ g\\
f_{0}^{\prime}\circ g&\dots&f_{n}^{\prime}\circ g\\
f_{0}^{\prime\prime}\circ g&\dots&f_{n}^{\prime\prime}\circ g\\
(f_{0}\circ g)^{\prime\prime\prime}&\dots&(f_{n}\circ g)^{\prime\prime\prime}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
(f_{0}\cdot g)^{(n)}&\dots&(f_{n}\circ g)^{(n)}\end{pmatrix}.

Proceeding in this manner with

(f_{i}\circ g)^{(k)}=(g^{\prime})^{(k)}\cdot(f_{i}^{(k)}\circ g)+{\dots}+g^{(k)}\cdot(f_{i}^{\prime}\circ g)

we get

\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{n})=(g^{\prime})^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}\cdot\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})\circ g

with proves the assertion.

{sol}

prob:wronskiReduction

Proof of
5.9

Set
\mathcal{H}=\{h_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
with
h_{i}:=\frac{f_{i}}{f_{0}}
.
Then by
5.7
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{n+1}\cdot\mathcal{W}(h_{0},\dots,h_{n})

and since
h_{0}=1
we have
h_{0}^{\prime}=h_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\dots=0
and

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{n+1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{1}&\dots&h_{n}\\
0&h_{1}^{\prime}&\dots&h_{n}^{\prime}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
0&h_{1}^{(n)}&\dots&h_{n}^{(n)}\end{pmatrix}

which gives by expanding along the first column

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{n+1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}^{\prime}&\dots&h_{n}^{\prime}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
h_{1}^{(n)}&\dots&h_{n}^{(n)}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{n+1}\cdot\mathcal{W}(h_{1}^{\prime},\dots,h_{n}^{\prime})

and with
g_{i}=h_{i+1}^{\prime}
for
i=0,\dots,n-1
we get

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{n+1}\cdot\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{n-1})

which proves the statement.

{sol}

prob:ecRestriction

(a)
Since
\mathcal{F}
is an ET-system on
[a,b]
we have

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})(x)\neq 0

for all
x\in[a,b]
, i.e., also for all
x\in[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]\subseteq[a,b]
and hence it is an ET-system on
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
.

(b)
Since
\mathcal{F}
is an ECT-system on
[a,b]
we have

\mathcal{W}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})(x)\neq 0

for all
x\in[a,b]
and
k=0,\dots,n
, i.e., also for all
x\in[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]\subseteq[a,b]
and
k=0,\dots,n
and hence it is an ECT-system on
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
.

{sol}

prob:xf

Proof of
4.19

We already know that
\{1,x,x^{2},\dots,x^{k}\}
is an ET-system for any
k=0,1,\dots,n
since

cW(1,x,\dots,x^{k})(x)=1\cdot 1!\cdot{\cdots}\cdot k!>0.

From the Wronskian determinant

\mathcal{W}(1,x,\dots,x^{n},f)(x)=1\cdot 1!\cdot 2!\cdot{\dots}\cdot n!\cdot f^{(n)}(x)>0

we then get that
\mathcal{F}
is an ECT-system on
[a,b]
by
5.12
.

prob:etSystExm

Proof of
5.18

By
5.8
we only need to prove the statement for one case, say case (b)
\mathcal{G}=\{e^{\alpha_{i}x}\}_{i=0}^{n}
.
Let
k\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(g_{0},\dots,g_{k})

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}g_{0}&g_{1}&\dots&g_{k}\\
g_{0}^{\prime}&g_{1}^{\prime}&\dots&g_{k}^{\prime}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
g_{0}^{(k)}&g_{1}^{(k)}&\dots&g_{k}^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}

and with
g_{i}^{(j)}=\alpha_{i}^{j}\cdot g_{i}
we get

\displaystyle=\deg\begin{pmatrix}g_{0}&g_{1}&\dots&g_{k}\\
\alpha_{0}g_{0}&\alpha_{1}g_{1}&\dots&\alpha_{k}g_{k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\alpha_{0}^{k}g_{0}&\alpha_{1}^{k}g_{1}&\dots&\alpha_{k}^{k}g_{k}\end{pmatrix}=g_{0}\cdot g_{1}\cdots g_{n}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}1&1&\dots&1\\
\alpha_{0}&\alpha_{1}&\dots&\alpha_{k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\alpha_{0}^{k}&\alpha_{1}^{k}&\dots&\alpha_{k}^{k}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=g_{0}\cdot g_{1}\cdots g_{k}\cdot\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq k}(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i})\neq 0

which proves the statement.

prob:exampleECTpolynomial
To construct the non-negative polynomial on
[0,\infty)
with the double zero
x_{1}=1
and the zero
x_{2}=2
with algebraic multiplicity
m_{2}=4
we need
7
monomials.
We chose
f_{0}(x)=1,f_{1}(x)=x^{2},f_{2}(x)=x^{3},f_{3}(x)=x^{5},f_{4}(x)=x^{8},f_{5}(x)=x^{11},f_{6}(x)=x^{13}
and leave out
x^{42}
.
With (
39
) we get

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cccccc}f_{0}&\,f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&f_{4}&f_{5}&f_{6}\\
x&\,1&1&2&2&2&2\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}(x)&f_{1}(x)&f_{2}(x)&f_{3}(x)&f_{4}(x)&f_{5}(x)&f_{6}(x)\\
f_{0}(x_{1})&f_{1}(x_{1})&f_{2}(x_{1})&f_{3}(x_{1})&f_{4}(x_{1})&f_{5}(x_{1})&f_{6}(x_{1})\\
f_{0}^{\prime}(x_{1})&f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})&f_{2}^{\prime}(x_{1})&f_{3}^{\prime}(x_{1})&f_{4}^{\prime}(x_{1})&f_{5}^{\prime}(x_{1})&f_{6}^{\prime}(x_{1})\\
f_{0}(x_{2})&f_{1}(x_{2})&f_{2}(x_{2})&f_{3}(x_{2})&f_{4}(x_{2})&f_{5}(x_{2})&f_{6}(x_{2})\\
f_{0}^{\prime}(x_{2})&f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{2})&f_{2}^{\prime}(x_{2})&f_{3}^{\prime}(x_{2})&f_{4}^{\prime}(x_{2})&f_{5}^{\prime}(x_{2})&f_{6}^{\prime}(x_{2})\\
f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{3}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{4}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{5}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{6}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})\\
f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{4}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{5}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{2})&f_{6}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{2})\\
f_{0}^{(4)}(x_{2})&f_{1}^{(4)}(x_{2})&f_{2}^{(4)}(x_{2})&f_{3}^{(4)}(x_{2})&f_{4}^{(4)}(x_{2})&f_{5}^{(4)}(x_{2})&f_{6}^{(4)}(x_{2})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{2}&x^{3}&x^{5}&x^{8}&x^{11}&x^{13}\\
1&1&1&1&1&1&1\\
0&2&3&5&8&11&13\\
1&4&8&32&256&2\,048&8\,192\\
0&4&12&80&1\,024&11\,264&53\,248\\
0&2&12&160&3\,584&56\,320&319\,488\\
0&0&6&240&10\,752&253\,440&1\,757\,184\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=48\cdot\left(14\,980\,788x^{13}-184\,325\,420x^{11}+2\,421\,354\,616x^{8}-26\,336\,028\,160x^{5}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\,\left.+112\,945\,898\,496x^{3}-112\,347\,781\,120x^{2}+23\,485\,900\,800\right).

The function
f
is shown in
Figure

3
.

Figure 3
:
The function
f
from the solution of Problem
5
.

This function
f
we gave here is not unique.
Of course every multiple of
f
also fulfills the requirements but we also made the restrictions to use all monomials except
x^{42}
.
We get another polynomial when we e.g. leave out
x^{13}
(or any other monomial except
1
) instead of
x^{42}
.
Then any conic linear combination of these functions also fulfills the requirements.

We can not leave out
1
since any linear combination has the additional zero
x=0
.

prob:smoothing

Proof of
6.8

Since
\mathcal{F}
is a continuous T-system we can assume that

\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&\dots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}>0

for all
a\leq x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}\leq b
.
Since the Gaussian kernel is ETP
k
for every
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, see
6.6
,
we have

K_{\sigma}^{*}\!\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{n}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&\dots&y_{n}\end{pmatrix}>0

for all
x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{n}
and
y_{1}\leq y_{2}\leq\dots\leq y_{n}
in
\mathds{R}
as well as
\sigma>0
.
Hence, in
\mathcal{W}(f_{\sigma,0},f_{\sigma,1},\dots,f_{\sigma,n})(x)=
(
54
) in
6.7
we are integrating over a non-negative functions with respect to the Lebesgue measure
\mu=\lambda
, i.e.,
\mathcal{W}(f_{\sigma,0},f_{\sigma,1},\dots,f_{\sigma,n})(x)>0
for all
x\in[a,b]
which proves the statement.

prob:posR

Proof of
8.4
on
\mathds{R}

By (a) there exists a function
w\in\mathcal{C}(\mathds{R},\mathds{R})
such that
w>0
on
\mathds{R}
and

\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{f_{n}(x)}{w(x)}=1.

By (b) we define

v_{i}(x):=\begin{cases}\frac{f_{i}(x)}{w(x)}&\text{if}\ x\in\mathds{R},\\
\delta_{i,n}&\text{if}\ x=\pm\infty\end{cases}

for all
i=0,1,\dots,n
.
Then by (c) and
4.9
we have that
\{v_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system on
[0,\infty]
.
With
t(x):=\tan(\pi x/2)
we define

g_{i}(x):=v_{i}\circ t

for all
i=0,1,\dots,n
.
Hence,
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is a T-system on
[-1,1]
by
4.8
.
We now apply
7.3
to
\mathcal{G}
. Set
g:=(\frac{f}{w})\circ t
.

(ii): By
7.3
on
[a,b]
there exist points

-1=y_{0}<x_{1}<y_{1}<\dots<x_{m}<y_{m}=1

and unique functions
g_{*}
and
g^{*}
such that
g=g_{*}+g^{*}
,
g_{*},g^{*}\geq 0
on
[-1,1]
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
are the zeros of
g_{*}
, and
y_{0},\dots,y_{m}
are the zeros of
g^{*}
.
Then
f_{*}:=(g_{*}\circ t^{-1})\cdot w
and
f^{*}:=(g^{*}\circ t^{-1})\cdot w
are the unique components in the decomposition
f=f_{*}+f^{*}
.

(i): Since
g^{*}(y_{0})=g^{*}(y_{m})=0
we have that
g^{*}
contains no
g_{2m}
and hence the coefficient of
g_{2m}
in
g_{*}
is
a_{2m}
.

{sol}

prob:nonnegR

Proof of
8.5
on
\mathds{R}

Similar to the proof of
8.3
on
[0,\infty)
and hence Problem/Solution
8
.

The conditions (a) – (c) are such that
\mathcal{F}
on
[-\infty,\infty]
, i.e., including
\pm\infty
, is an ET-system.

With the same argument as in the proof of
8.1
we transform
\mathcal{F}
on
[-\infty,\infty]
into
\mathcal{G}
on
[-1,1]
with the
\tan
-function.
Here
5.8
ensures that also
\mathcal{G}
is an ET-system.

Application of
7.6
on
[-1,1]
gives the desired decomposition
g=g_{*}+g^{*}
with the observation that
x=\pm 1
is a zero of at most multiplicity one by (a) and (b).
Backwards transformation into
\mathcal{F}
on
[-\infty,\infty]
resp.
[-\infty,\infty)
then gives the assertion.

prob:a0

Proof of
9.13

9.10
can in general not be extended to
[0,b]
since
\{x^{\alpha_{0}},\dots,x^{\alpha_{n}}\}
is not an ET-system.
This fails at
x=0
.
But on
(0,b]
it is an ET-system.
We can therefore factor out the zeros of
f\geq 0
at
x=0

f(x)=a_{i}x^{\alpha_{i}}+a_{i+1}x^{\alpha_{i+1}}+\dots+a_{n}x^{\alpha_{n}}=x^{\alpha_{i}}\cdot(\underbrace{a_{i}+a_{i+1}x^{\alpha_{i+1}-\alpha_{i}}+\dots+a_{n}x^{\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{i}}}_{=:\tilde{f}(x)})

to get some
\tilde{f}
with
\tilde{f}\geq 0
on
[0,b]
and
\tilde{f}(0)>0
.
To
\tilde{f}
we can then apply
9.10
with
a=0
.

In summary,
9.10
on
[0,b]
holds if
f(0)>0
, see also
10.5
and
10.6
for the corresponding version on
[0,\infty)
.

prob:nonneg0infty

Proof of
10.5

To prove
10.5
we have to note that
\mathcal{F}=\{x^{\alpha_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
with
\alpha_{0}=1
is an ET-system on
(0,\infty)
.
The only difficulty is
x=0
where
\mathcal{F}
fails to be a ET-system.

But looking closely at the proof of
7.5
(see Problem/Solution
7
) the ET-system property is only required in a neighborhood of the zeros of
f
and otherwise it is the proof of
7.1
for T-systems.
Since
f(0)>0
we have no zero at
x=0
where
\mathcal{F}
fails to be a T-system.
In fact, we have
f(x)>0
for all
x\in[0,\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
.
Hence, we can apply
8.3
since its proof requires for our
f
with
f(0)>0
only that
\mathcal{F}
to be an ET-system on
(0,\infty)
which is fulfilled.

prob:leadingcoeffR
By expanding

\prod_{i=1}^{r}(x-z_{i})^{m_{i}}\cdot\left(a\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x-x_{i})^{2}+b\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(x-y_{i})^{2}\right)

we see that
a\cdot x^{m_{1}+\dots+m_{r}+2m}
is the monomial with the highest degree
m_{1}+\dots+m_{r}+2m=\deg p
and the coefficient is
a
.
